Two further bounds had nothing at all: an **inverted** `min_qty`/`max_qty` pair admits no quantity, so the add-on is unselectable while the plan publishes clean and sells; a **zero or negative** `step_qty` leaves the admissible quantity set undefined. The implementers added both as CHECKs because §5 gave the pipeline no code and nothing else could reject them, and reported them as additions §6 does not name rather than passing them off as the design's.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — one rule, one code, three bounds. **`ADDON_QTY_RANGE_INVALID` (422**, the offending bound named**)** covers `required ⇒ maxQty ≥ 1`, `minQty ≤ maxQty` when both are set, and `stepQty > 0` when set. They are one call because they are one defect — *these bounds admit no selection* — on one row, with one operator action: fix the bound the report names. It is the sibling of `PURCHASE_QTY_RANGE_INVALID` one object over (that one guards the plan's purchase window, this one the add-on's), which is why the name follows it rather than inventing a shape. §6 keeps all three as CHECKs, and the two the implementation added are now **named there**: the rule is the explanatory path and the constraint the guarantee, which is the read-then-index arrangement D-148 states for the scope key. Rejected: (a) leaving the bounds physical and calling the driver error sufficient — it is exactly what this slice's report contract forbids, and it fails a **required** add-on's author with a constraint name they cannot map to a field; (b) three codes, one per bound, which would discriminate where the remedy does not differ — D-146's line again. Not flagged for veto: no bound moved, and each of the three refusals was already implied by the field it constrains.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): the rule itself — G4 built the three CHECKs and has no pipeline rule to report them, which is the whole defect this entry closes.
- **Propagated**: S2 §3 `inst-cmp-addons` + §5 (the new code) + §6 key constraints (the two unnamed CHECKs named) + §8 `dod-composition` + §9; PRD §17.3 (a new **Add-on quantity bounds** rule row) + `fr-addon-rules`.

#### D-151 [M] `displayTrialDays` is a `trial`-phase field, and a drift CHECK cannot be what guarantees it

- **Where**: [`design/02-plan-definition.md`](./design/02-plan-definition.md) §3 `inst-ph-trial`, §5, §6 (the `display_trial_days` column note + the key-constraints paragraph), §8 `dod-phases`, §9; [PRD](./PRD.md) §1.4 glossary (**Plan phase**) + `fr-plan-phases`.
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-03 while building `pricing_plan_phase`): §6 guards the projection with `CHECK (display_trial_days IS NULL OR display_trial_days = phase_duration_days)`, and it is the only thing standing behind a field Subscriptions enforces trial runtime from and preview quotes. It cannot hold as written, twice over. **First, NULL propagation**: when `phase_duration_days` is NULL and `display_trial_days` is set, the comparison evaluates to NULL, which both engines count as **satisfied** — so the exact shape the CHECK exists to forbid, a phase publishing a trial length it does not have, passes it. **Second, and worse, the CHECK says nothing about the phase `kind` and neither does any rule**: `inst-ph-trial` states what a `trial` phase publishes and forbids nothing elsewhere, so an `intro` phase, or the `evergreen` **terminal** phase, may carry a `displayTrialDays` of its own. The terminal phase is where the two holes meet and where nothing catches them: `inst-ph-duration` is *correct* to find no duration there (a terminal phase must carry none) and `inst-ph-graph`'s C-4 rule is *correct* to find `evergreen`, so a plan with no trial phase at all publishes a trial length that preview quotes and Subscriptions runs. The implementers implemented the CHECK verbatim and refused at publish instead, correctly — and the publish-side rules they named cover only the non-terminal case.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — the field is axis-conditioned and gets a rule and a code, which is `inst-el-fields`' treatment one slice over (**D-147**: one field conditioned on one axis value, a constraint with no rule getting no code and therefore surfacing as an internal fault). `displayTrialDays` is authorable **only** on a phase whose `kind` is `trial`, and only on one carrying the `phaseDurationDays` it projects; anything else fails publish **`DISPLAY_TRIAL_DAYS_INVALID` (422**, the phase named**)**. The §6 CHECK **stays exactly as written**, NULL propagation and all, and the entry says so deliberately: tightening it with a `phase_duration_days IS NOT NULL` conjunct would make the half-authored draft unsavable, and a phase graph is authored across successive `PATCH`es — successor in one request, duration in the next — so the schema stands *behind* the pipeline here exactly as it already does for `inst-ph-duration`, the terminal `kind`, and the absent FK on `converts_to_phase_id`. Rejected: (a) tightening the CHECK, for the reason above — it trades a publish-time report line for an authoring-time refusal of a legal intermediate state; (b) stretching `PHASE_DURATION_INVALID`, which is the duration rule and would tell an author their duration is wrong when their duration is right and their **projection** is the fault; (c) reading `displayTrialDays` as a free per-phase display field, which the unqualified wording admits — rejected because the PRD makes it *the* trial length for preview and the single source Subscriptions enforces from, and a plan-level fact sourced from any phase that cares to set one has no defined value at all. Not flagged for veto — every document that mentions the field already scopes it to `trial` phases; nothing narrows, one silence closes.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): the rule; G4's phase-graph set has no `DISPLAY_TRIAL_DAYS_INVALID`, and its migration doc records the residual shape as refused at publish by two rules that do not in fact reach the terminal-phase case.
- **Propagated**: S2 §3 `inst-ph-trial` + §5 (the new code) + §6 (the column note + the key-constraints paragraph, which now says why the CHECK is kept as written) + §8 `dod-phases` + §9; PRD §1.4 glossary (**Plan phase**) + `fr-plan-phases`.

#### D-152 [M] The gear's per-tenant configurables get a carrier — `pricing_policy_object`

- **Where**: [`design/01-foundation.md`](./design/01-foundation.md) §3.7 (`pricing_policy_object`); [`design/02-plan-definition.md`](./design/02-plan-definition.md) §1.6 P1 + P5, §3 `inst-ds-sufficient`, §6 (`pricing_plan_descriptor_set`), §8 `dod-descriptors`, §9; [PRD](./PRD.md) §7.1 `nfr-size-limits` + §14 (the ratified-NFR assumption) + `fr-billing-descriptors`.
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-03 while building the descriptor rule): `inst-ds-sufficient` and P5 promise that "the validator's required-set is **config-extensible** without a schema change", and the descriptor set has a value carrier for the extension (`additional_fields`). **Nothing anywhere says where the extended required-set itself is declared** — not §6, not §10, not PRD §14, and not the gear's configuration section. As it stands `DESCRIPTOR_INCOMPLETE` can only ever check the pinned v1 three, so the promise describes a capability with no configuration to exercise it: when Billing countersigns and asks for a fourth field, the "without a schema change" clause buys nothing. The same shape holds one layer up, and is why this is one entry: **four ratified numbers are called tenant-configurable and none of them has a carrier either** — the tier-band and price-row soft caps (100 / 500) and the two custom-interval caps (366 days / 24 months), each ratified 2026-07-28 with that adjective, and the gear's own configuration section is per **deployment**, so every tenant of a deployment shares one cap. A promise of per-tenant configuration that names no store is the D-01 class: a check with no data source.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — the carrier is **`pricing_policy_object`**, the Foundation-owned per-tenant policy store that already holds the approval-threshold policy, the tax-display policy, the default rounding policy and the enforced-migration notice period. Two clauses, one call. (1) The **descriptor required-set extension** is an entry there: additional required descriptor keys, matched against `pricing_plan_descriptor_set.additional_fields`, additive-only over the pinned v1 three, enforced by the existing **`DESCRIPTOR_INCOMPLETE`** — **no new code**, a missing extended key being a missing descriptor element like any other. (2) The **four §14 caps** are entries there too; a tenant with no entry takes the ratified launch default, so nothing about the ratified values moves. Nothing here is on a resolution path — these are authoring-time policy reads, exactly like the two that were already in that bullet. Rejected: (a) a **per-deployment** configuration section, which is what the code actually built and which contradicts the adjective in a ratified NFR — and, for the descriptor set specifically, would let one deployment's tenants share a Billing contract they do not share; (b) **dropping "config-extensible"** from P5 and pinning the required-set at v1 until a D-48 v2 — cheap, honest, and it removes the one property that makes the pinned set safe to pin, since the alternative to configuring a fourth required key is a schema change on a table Billing's countersign is still pending against; (c) a **new policy table** for the descriptor set alone, which would give one gear two per-tenant policy stores with no rule for which holds what. **Flagged for veto** — it fixes **tenant** scope for a required-set that is part of Billing's pinned contract, so it decides who may tighten that contract and at what granularity; the built code's per-deployment section is recorded as the debt it now owes. **CONFIRMED as decided by the product owner 2026-08-03**, against alternatives (a) and (b) above: a per-deployment section would let one deployment's tenants share a Billing contract they do not share, and dropping "config-extensible" would pin the required-set at v1 behind a schema change on the very table whose countersign is still pending. **The carrier is provisional, and the owner qualified the confirmation to say so**: `pricing_policy_object` holds the descriptor required-set extension and the four caps **for now**, and these settings are expected to move to a **settings gear** when one exists. **That gear does not exist in this repository, and this note is a placeholder for one.** It is written down because the alternative is that this entry reads, in six months, as the claim that a per-tenant cap *belongs* in a pricing gear's policy table, and the migration then arrives as a surprise. `gears/simple-user-settings` is **not** the referent and must not be mistaken for it (checked 2026-08-03): its rows are keyed `(tenant_id, user_id)`, so every entry hangs off a **user** and there is no per-tenant row for a tenant-wide cap to occupy; the shipped table is two fixed nullable columns (`theme`, `language`) rather than the free-form store even its own PRD describes; and that PRD puts "settings validation schemas" and versioning/history explicitly **out of scope**, so it can neither type a policy entry nor keep the change history a cap on a Billing-facing contract needs. It shares the word and nothing else. Before these entries could move, the destination would have to have: a per-**tenant** scope (not per-user); a typed schema for policy entries, since a cap is a bounded integer and the required-set an additive key list over a pinned v1 three, and an untyped blob would move the validation this gear performs into a store that promises none; and the authoring authz and audit these reads inherit here from Foundation §2.2 + `inst-rb-pep`, because who may tighten Billing's required-set is exactly what this decision fixed. Until then this bullet is the carrier, and the §3.7 bullet carries the same clause.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): the caps move from the per-deployment configuration section to a per-tenant policy read (the section stays as the default source), and the descriptor rule gains the extended-key lookup. G4 declined to invent a `descriptors.required_keys` config field, which is why this entry exists rather than a fait accompli.
- **Propagated**: S1 §3.7 (`pricing_policy_object` bullet); S2 §1.6 P1 + P5 + §3 `inst-ds-sufficient` + §6 (`pricing_plan_descriptor_set`) + §8 `dod-descriptors` + §9; PRD §7.1 (`nfr-size-limits` threshold) + §14 (the ratified-NFR assumption) + `fr-billing-descriptors`.

#### D-153 [M] The price row's draft plane is transition-guarded too (D-148 completion)

- **Where**: [`design/01-foundation.md`](./design/01-foundation.md) §3.7 (`pricing_price` append-only paragraph); [`design/03-price-structure.md`](./design/03-price-structure.md) §4 (`inst-ps-draft-edges`, new).
- **Problem** (found while building G3, carried forward, and still true after G4): §3.7's append-only enforcement is a **column whitelist**, and a column whitelist is scoped to *published* rows by construction — it says nothing about where a **draft** row may go. The price row's state machine has exactly one edge out of `draft`, to `published` (S3 §4), and nothing physical held it: `draft → superseded` was a permitted UPDATE. The same class was closed on `pricing_plan`, whose trigger constrains its draft rows' outbound flips (`draft → draft | published | abandoned`, D-145), and this is the table it was not closed on. What the flip costs is precise: both scope-key partial `UNIQUE` indexes are predicated on a **single** `lifecycle_state` — `published` for one, `draft` for the other (D-148) — so a row moved straight to `superseded` leaves both, and its canonical scope key reads **free on both planes**. The draft-plane guarantee D-148 had just bought is then undone by one UPDATE; `inst-ps-nodelete` makes the resulting row undeletable; and no supersession chain reaches it, because it was never current. Strictly less severe than the plan-side hole D-145 closed — a row that becomes superseded for nothing was never current, so no consumer resolves it and no money moves — but it is the same class and belongs beside it.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — the trigger constrains the draft row's `lifecycle_state` as well: `draft → draft | published`, and nothing else, mirroring `pricing_plan`'s own guard over its own state set. Stated as an instruction on the state machine that owns the edges (`inst-ps-draft-edges`) and as the physical clause in §3.7 that enforces it. **No new code**: no endpoint offers the transition and no caller can provoke it, so a wire code would document an API a client cannot reach — the line D-146 drew for the read model's pin frontier. This is the physical floor under a machine the engine already honours, the same posture D-148's index takes. Rejected: (a) leaving it to the engine's transition path on the argument that only the repositories write these rows — the argument D-148 rejected one clause earlier, since ad-hoc SQL and a future writer are exactly what an index or a trigger is for, and here the cost of being wrong is the silent loss of a uniqueness guarantee rather than a visible error; (b) widening a partial `UNIQUE` predicate to `IN ('draft', 'published', 'superseded')` so the row cannot escape, which would make every legitimate superseded predecessor collide with its successor. Not flagged for veto — nothing consumer-visible moves.
- **Propagated**: S1 §3.7 (`pricing_price` append-only paragraph); S3 §4 (`inst-ps-draft-edges`).

#### D-154 [M] The row-borne `taxCategory` is the resolved effective category, frozen with the row

- **Where**: [`design/02-plan-definition.md`](./design/02-plan-definition.md) §1.6 P5 + §3 `inst-ds-required` + §8 `dod-descriptors`; [`design/04-currency-tax.md`](./design/04-currency-tax.md) §3 `inst-td-persist` + `inst-td-policy`, §5, §6; [`design/01-foundation.md`](./design/01-foundation.md) §3.7 (`pricing_price`); [PRD](./PRD.md) §6.4 (the tax-display rule) + `fr-billing-descriptors`.
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-03 while building the descriptor rule, which covers **three** of D-48 v1's five elements — the other two ride the price row and are checked by nobody in this crate, since neither Slice 4's nor Slice 6's storage has landed). `billingTiming` is fine on paper: S6 `inst-bt-required` registers it with `BILLING_TIMING_MISSING`, single owner, cross-referenced here. `taxCategory` is not, and the gap is in the documents rather than in the build. `inst-ds-required` says publish "blocks on any missing element", and PRD `fr-billing-descriptors` makes it a **MUST** — while S4 `inst-td-policy` evaluates the **effective** category (`coalesce(row.tax_category_ref, readiness.taxCategory)`) and puts its absence under the **tenant tax-display policy**, whose `warn` mode is explicitly allowed. So a tenant on `warn` publishes a row with no tax category anywhere and the pinned v1 element is simply absent from what Billing receives: two documents, one required element, opposite answers. Separately and unconditionally, the descriptor contract promises a set "sufficient for Billing/ERP to post **without re-querying mutable catalog rows**" — and the fallback half of this value lives on `pricing_region_taxonomy`, which is tenant-declared, mutable and re-declarable at any time (`inst-td-readiness`). Freezing the raw column alone therefore hands Billing an element it must re-resolve against a row that can move under a frozen `CatalogVersion`.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — the required element is the **effective** category, and publish **resolves** it and freezes the **result** with the row, exactly as it already resolves and freezes `rounding_policy_ref` (§3.7 / PRD §17.4). Two consequences, stated rather than implied. (1) A row whose effective category resolves to **nothing** fails publish (`TAX_BASIS_INCOMPLETE`) **whatever the tenant policy says**: the policy keeps the `ratePresent=false` arm, where the missing fact is a rate nobody in this gear owns and Tax Engine is pre-GA, and loses the category arm, because D-48 v1 **pins** that element and a per-tenant display policy may not publish past a pinned contract element. The two arms had been one sentence and read as one switch; they are not the same kind of fact. (2) The authored column is untouched and remains the row's source of truth (D-110) — what changes is that no consumer re-derives the fallback. Rejected: (a) making `tax_category_ref` **unconditionally required per row**, which would delete the coalesce rule S4 has confirmed twice and force every tenant to author a category on every row to say what one region-level default already says; (b) **narrowing D-48** so `taxCategory` is not a publish-blocking element, which is the other way to make the two documents agree and which quietly removes a pinned element from a contract Billing has not yet countersigned — the direction that costs least to write and most to discover; (c) leaving the freeze at the raw column and letting Billing resolve, which is the sufficiency promise reversed. **Flagged for veto** — clause (1) removes an arm from a tenant-configurable policy the PRD states covers both, and the descriptor set is the contract Billing countersigns, so both halves are the owner's to weigh. **CONFIRMED as decided by the product owner 2026-08-03, on both halves and with no qualification**, against alternatives (a) and (b) above: (a) an unconditional per-row `tax_category_ref` deletes the coalesce rule S4 has confirmed twice and makes every author restate on every row what one region-level default already says, and (b) narrowing D-48 is the cheapest way to make the two documents agree and the most expensive to discover, since it quietly drops a pinned element from a contract its counterparty has not yet countersigned. The ground the owner confirmed on is the one this entry states and did not move: publish resolves the effective category and freezes the **result** with the row, and a row whose effective category resolves to nothing fails publish (`TAX_BASIS_INCOMPLETE`) **whatever the tenant tax-display policy says**. The two arms are not one switch — the policy keeps `ratePresent=false`, where the missing fact is a rate no one in this gear owns and Tax Engine is pre-GA, and loses the category arm, because a pinned contract element is not a display preference and a per-tenant policy may not publish past one.
- **Owed back to the implementation**: nothing in G4 — neither slice's storage exists — but Slice 4's build owes the resolution and the freeze, and Slice 6's owes `BILLING_TIMING_MISSING`; the descriptor rule's three-of-five coverage is thereby total rather than deferred, and is recorded here so the next slice inherits the obligation rather than rediscovering it.
- **Propagated**: S1 §3.7 (`pricing_price` bullet — the resolved freeze beside `rounding_policy_ref`'s); S2 §1.6 P5 + §3 `inst-ds-required` + §8 `dod-descriptors`; S4 §3 `inst-td-persist` + `inst-td-policy` + §5 (the code's two arms) + §6; PRD §6.4 (the tax-display rule) + `fr-billing-descriptors`.

#### D-155 [H] The publish commit flips exactly the row set its own re-validation judged

- **Where**: [`design/01-foundation.md`](./design/01-foundation.md) §4.2 (the publish contract — step 4 and the new commit-subject paragraph); [`design/03-price-structure.md`](./design/03-price-structure.md) §4 (`inst-ps-publish`).
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-03 while building G5, the publish commit): §4.2 step 2 says the pre-check's rule set **re-runs inside the publish-commit transaction**, and step 4 says the commit "transitions the row set" — and **no document says which row set**. A commit that re-reads the plan's `draft` rows at flip time is a conforming reading of both sentences, and it is the reading that was built first. Between the assembly the rules judge and the flip sits the registry round-trip (D-156), and the transaction runs at the engine default isolation, where every statement takes a fresh snapshot. In that window a concurrent `create_draft` publishes a row **the rule set never saw**, and a concurrent `update_draft` publishes a mutation of a row it had passed — under a validation contract whose entire promise is that nothing unvalidated reaches production (`fr-publish-validation-failclosed`). The set had the token to close it since D-141 and no rule saying what to spend it on. The near miss is worth recording with the fix: the argument that talked implementers out of looking was sound *and about a different invariant* — `(tenant_id, chain_id, seq)`, the outbox sequence and `uq_pricing_plan_current` do make their forks unrepresentable at any isolation level, but a key can only refuse a second row at a position it already knows about. **A conclusion about predicates does not follow from a premise about keys.**
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — the commit's subject is **pinned by identity and version**, and three clauses say what that means. **(1) The flip applies to exactly the `(price_id, row_version)` pairs the re-run judged.** A row whose version moved is refused naming the row (**`STALE_VERSION`**, §3.3 — **no new code**, this is precisely what D-141 gave every price row its own tag for); a row that did not exist at assembly is not in the set, stays `draft`, and publishes with the next revision. The pre-check deliberately does **not** pin the subject — a row authored between pre-check and commit *is* judged, by the second run — so the pinning is a property of the commit transaction alone. **(2) Every other input the rule set reads is held by a stated mechanism, and the list is closed:** the draft revision row by its own compare-and-swap; its Slice-2 children (phases, add-on rules, descriptor set) by that same revision tag, which every child edit bumps; the plan's **published** price rows' *content* by `pricing_price`'s append-only trigger; the current published revision row by `pricing_plan`'s frozen-column whitelist; that revision's phase set by `pricing_plan_phase`'s append-only trigger; the tenant policy row by **nothing** — a policy edit committing afterwards is staleness, not a bypass, the next publish enforcing the new value, and this gear has no writer for that row. `SERIALIZABLE` is therefore **not required** and is declined on its merits rather than for want of a mechanism: it would buy nothing the tags and triggers above do not, and it would hold predicate locks across the registry round-trip D-156 places inside this transaction — turning a slow remote service into blocked publishes. **(3) One entry in that list is a premise, not a mechanism, and the design set carries it as one.** The append-only trigger guarantees a published row's *content*; it does **not** guarantee the *membership* of the published set, because the same trigger sanctions `published → superseded`. Four completeness rules range over that set (`inst-ph-coverage`, `inst-cs-hybrid`, `inst-cs-usage`, and D-84's per-market usage rule) and the in-use phase id set is derived from it, so a row leaving it between assembly and commit changes what was judged. What holds it **today** is that the gear has no producer for that flip at all: its two sanctioned producers are the **D-88** supersession unit and the **D-100** cutover, both Slice 7's, and neither is built. **The group that builds either one deletes this premise**, and then owes the published half a membership guard of its own — the same `(price_id, row_version)` shape extends to it, published rows' tags being frozen with their content (D-141), or the membership is pinned some other way. Rejected: (a) re-deriving the set at flip time — the pre-fix behaviour, and the whole defect; (b) raising isolation instead, which is a property of a schedule rather than a predicate on a statement and is therefore unprovable at the level where it would be asserted, while costing exactly the thing the step ordering was chosen to bound; (c) leaving the premise in clause (3) implicit on the grounds that no producer exists, which is how a later slice deletes a guarantee nobody told it about — the same shape of unstated "all" that hid the original defect. Not flagged for veto — nothing consumer-visible moves; this states what "the commit re-validates" already had to mean.
- **Propagated**: S1 §4.2 (step 4 + the commit-subject paragraph, incl. the Slice-7 obligation); S3 §4 `inst-ps-publish`.

#### D-156 [M] The `CatalogVersion` request happens inside the commit, after re-validation and before the writes

- **Where**: [`design/01-foundation.md`](./design/01-foundation.md) §3.6 (`seq-author-publish-fnd`); §4.2 (step 4).
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-03 while building G5): §3.6's sequence has the row set transition, then "the EventOutbox enqueues `PlanPublished` with a **pending** version ref, and the SnapshotStamper requests a `CatalogVersion`" — the enqueue **before** the request — while §4.2 step 4 requires the enqueued `PlanPublished` to **carry** that pending ref, and `fr-pricing-snapshot` requires publish to stamp it. Both cannot hold: at the moment §3.6 enqueues, there is no handle to put in the payload. One of the two documents describes a sequence that cannot be built, and no reader could tell which.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — §4.2 wins and §3.6 is corrected to match, because the payload requirement is the load-bearing half (a consumer reads the pending ref; nothing reads the request's position). The request is made **inside the publish transaction**, **after** the commit-time re-validation passes and **before** any write. Earlier is worse: a commit-time validation failure would leave a pending ref at the registry that nothing ever commits, tripping `pricing.catalogversion.commit_overdue` for a publish that never happened — an alarm §3.6 defines for the opposite condition. Later is impossible: the outbox payload would have nothing to carry. The cost is real and is named rather than hidden — **a network round-trip inside an open database transaction** — and it is bounded two ways. The `request_id` is **derived deterministically from `(tenant_id, plan_id, revision)`**, so a transaction that rolls back and retries re-requests the *same* handle instead of orphaning one, and any handle found pending is attributable to the unit that asked for it. And the one **permanent** post-request refusal is moved ahead of the request: a retired predecessor can never publish, so no retry would ever collect that handle — every other post-request refusal self-heals through the derived id. What remains orphanable is the race in which a concurrent commit publishes this very revision between the two runs, which §3.6's existing remediation covers ("a registry re-request, never a silent re-emit"). Rejected: (a) correcting §4.2 to §3.6 instead — it would remove the pending ref from `PlanPublished`, and the pending-then-committed handle is what `fr-pricing-snapshot` and the whole D-47 batching model rest on; (b) requesting outside the transaction, before it opens — the failure it avoids is the one above, and it re-opens the orphan on every validation refusal rather than on a race; (c) a random `request_id`, under which every retry strands a handle. Not flagged for veto — this removes a contradiction and moves no contract.
- **Propagated**: S1 §3.6 (the sequence, incl. the round-trip cost and the derived request id); §4.2 (step 4).

#### D-157 [M] The pending-version-ref row carries the subject its publish unit projects

- **Where**: [`design/01-foundation.md`](./design/01-foundation.md) §3.7 (`pricing_catalog_version_ref`).
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-03 while building G5): §3.7 describes the table in one clause — "`pending` vs `committed` version linkage per publish" — and names **no columns at all**, while §3.7's own `pricing_read_model` rule requires a version's rows to be "exactly the subjects of the publish units that produced it" (D-86/D-91) and §4.4 makes the projector responsible for writing them. The projector arrives at `CatalogVersionPublished` holding **committed refs**. With no subject on the ref row there is no path at all from a handle back to what it published, so the projected-row-set rule is unimplementable one section away from where it is stated — the D-01 class inside a single document.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — the ref row carries `subject_kind` and `subject_ref`, drawn from the **same universe as `pricing_read_model`'s** (`plan | price_overlay | overlay_index | group_membership`, extensible) rather than a second spelling of the same aggregates, so the projector's input and its output are keyed alike. Rejected: (a) the **outbox row** as the carrier — its `aggregate_id` is the plan and its payload holds the handle, so the join exists, but that makes a delivery queue the projector's durable index and the first compaction of delivered history removes the projector's input; (b) re-deriving the subject set from the truth tables at `CatalogVersionPublished` — it re-reads a world that has moved on, which is the same defect D-155 closes one clause over. **Owed forward, and stated so the slice that lands it inherits the obligation rather than rediscovering it:** one column pair holds **one** subject, and an overlay publish unit projects **two** — the overlay document *and* the D-112/D-133 `overlay_index` shard — so Slice 9's build widens this to a subject **set** (an ordered child table or a typed array), and D-133's two-shard case makes it three rows for one handle when a revision moves the overlay's scope value. Not flagged for veto — nothing consumer-visible moves; a rule that could not be implemented becomes implementable.
- **Propagated**: S1 §3.7 (`pricing_catalog_version_ref` bullet). **Extended by D-165** (2026-08-03) with `subject_revision` and `subject_lifecycle_state`, on the same argument: the content coordinates a publish judged are properties of the **publish unit**, exactly as the subject is, and D-86/D-91's keying of `pricing_read_model` is untouched by either.

#### D-158 [M] The audit log's `action` and `subject_kind` have a declared vocabulary, and it is the approval store's

- **Where**: [`design/05-governance.md`](./design/05-governance.md) §6 (`pricing_audit_log` writer contract); [`design/01-foundation.md`](./design/01-foundation.md) §3.7 (`pricing_audit_log`).
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-03 while building G5, the table's first writer): both discriminators are free text and **nothing in the design set declares either enumeration**. S5 §6 declares a `subject_kind` enum for `pricing_approval` — and did so deliberately, the 2026-07-31d review fix C-4 having found the kind "living only inside `materiality` jsonb, leaving the store with no queryable subject type" — while the audit log, which names the *same* aggregates and is the store D-12 confines to the Auditor, got none. Two consequences, and the first is not hypothetical: the implementation had to choose a token for a plan revision with no document to take it from, and chose to **borrow** the approval store's spelling precisely because the alternative was two names for one aggregate in two tables of one gear. The second is that `action` — the column that says what the record *is* — had no vocabulary at all, so the audit read surface (S12 `inst-he-read`'s Auditor-only sibling) could filter on nothing and D-14's tamper evidence hashed a field whose domain no reader could enumerate.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — both are declared, and `subject_kind` is **the approval store's enumeration verbatim** (`plan_revision | price_unit | window | overlay | membership | bundle | retirement | policy | historical_import | bulk_batch`, extensible), not a parallel one. The two stores discriminate the *same* aggregates for the same audience, and D-135 already keys the chain on the audited subject's aggregate, so a second vocabulary would mean the approval record and the audit record of one decision disagree on what the decision was about. `action` is a **declared, additive `snake_case` verb set**, opened with exactly the records this design set already requires somebody to write — `publish` (§4.2), `abandon` (D-145's audited flip), `retire` (S11 `inst-rt-*`), `approve` / `reject` (S5 `inst-tp-record`), `deny` (S5 `inst-rb-audit` and `inst-tp-selfaudit`'s attempted violation), `backdate_import` (S5 `inst-bd-*`), `policy_update` (D-10's threshold-policy mutations) — so a slice that adds an audited record adds its token here rather than inventing one at the keyboard. Two constraints on the set, both learned from the same near-collision: an action token is **never a frozen event name** (`PlanPublished` is a `CatalogEvent` with one home; the audit action for the same transaction is `publish`), and a token with no writer is not declared, because a vocabulary entry nobody writes reads as coverage to everyone who greps for it. Rejected: (a) an audit-log-specific `subject_kind` enumeration, which is the free-text status quo with a `CHECK` on it and leaves the two stores free to diverge; (b) reusing the **event** names as actions, which gives one string two homes and no name at all for the audited acts that emit no event — every denial, every self-approval attempt, the abandon flip; (c) leaving `action` free text and letting the reading surface discover the domain, which is what makes an audit column unqueryable at exactly the retention horizon (≥ 7 years) where nobody remembers what was written. **No new code** — this is a stored vocabulary, not a refusal. Not flagged for veto; naming what the set's own records already are decides nothing about them.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): G5 declared only the two values it writes (`publish`, `plan_revision`) and recorded the borrowing as a borrowing; with the vocabulary declared, the borrowed spelling is now the stated one, and later groups add their tokens as their records land.
- **Propagated**: S5 §6 (`pricing_audit_log` writer contract); S1 §3.7 (`pricing_audit_log` bullet).
- **Amended by D-175 (2026-08-03)**: the `action` set gains `create`, `update` and `delete`, and gains a **closure rule** — the opening enumeration above stated this vocabulary's *provenance* and was read as its *closure*, so five of six mutating authoring surfaces had a normative record and no token to write it under. "No token without a writer" now has its companion, "no writer without a token".

#### D-159 [M] A same-aggregate write contention is a retriable refusal with a code of its own

- **Where**: [`design/01-foundation.md`](./design/01-foundation.md) §3.3 (Problem responses + the conflict-class count); §3.7 (`pricing_audit_log`, `pricing_outbox`); [`design/05-governance.md`](./design/05-governance.md) §6 (`pricing_audit_log` writer contract).
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-03 while building G5): D-135 segments the audit chain per `(tenant_id, chain_id)` and the segment head is `MAX(seq)` under the primary key `(tenant_id, chain_id, seq)`, so two mutations of one aggregate that read the same head cannot both insert after it — the loser takes a unique violation and its **whole mutation transaction** rolls back. That is contention: not a corrupt row, not a caller mistake, and not a fault of the store. **No wire code names it**, so under the gear's own no-invented-codes discipline the refusal lands on an internal fault, where it is **indistinguishable from a dead connection** — a client is told to stop and page someone about a race whose entire remedy is to try again. The same shape holds at the gear's other two per-aggregate serialization points, the outbox's per-`(tenant_id, aggregate_id)` sequence and the current-revision partial `UNIQUE`, so the gap is the publish path's and not one table's.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — **`CONCURRENT_MUTATION` (409**, naming the aggregate**)**: another mutation of this aggregate committed first; the caller's request was well-formed, its preconditions held, and a retry is expected to succeed. It is classified by §3.3's own rule ("a retriable conflict on mutable state stays a **409**"), which is what makes it a sixth member of that class rather than a new kind of thing. It is deliberately **not** `STALE_VERSION`, whose remedy is *re-read and resubmit* because the caller's precondition genuinely failed — here nothing the caller presented was stale — and deliberately not `IDEMPOTENCY_KEY_IN_FLIGHT` (D-143), whose subject is the caller's **own** duplicate rather than another actor's mutation. D-146's line drawn twice over: the operator's next action differs from both. One code covers all three serialization points, by the same line — the action is identical at each, and the message names the aggregate. Rejected: (a) leaving it an internal fault, which is the status quo and costs a retriable request a page; (b) three codes, one per store, which discriminates where the remedy does not differ and exposes the gear's internal serialization points as a client-facing taxonomy; (c) making it a 5xx retry hint, which is a different contract — a 409 says the request may be replayed as sent, and it may.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): the distinguishable refusal itself. G5 recorded it as **unimplemented** rather than minting a code, and re-filed it out of the "owed to a Postgres suite" list where it had first been placed — a suite would demonstrate the race, but only a code makes its loser tellable from a dead connection.
- **Propagated**: S1 §3.3 (the new code + the conflict-class count) + §3.7 (`pricing_audit_log`, `pricing_outbox`); S5 §6 (the writer contract's contention clause).

#### D-160 [M] An advisory carries a code exactly as a violation does — the two the set states get theirs

- **Where**: [`design/01-foundation.md`](./design/01-foundation.md) §1.2 (NFR allocation) + §3.3 (Problem responses + the advisory-channel rule); [`design/03-price-structure.md`](./design/03-price-structure.md) §3 `inst-tb-order`; [PRD](./PRD.md) §7.1 `nfr-size-limits`.
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-03 while building G5's rule set): the validation report envelope is "blocking `violations[]` plus advisory `warnings[]`" (§3.3, ratified as R-11), the set states exactly **two** advisories, and it names a code for **neither**. `inst-tb-order`'s rising-unit-price warning has a rule, two carve-outs and a channel; `nfr-size-limits` says the system **SHOULD** enforce the tier-band and price-row soft caps "emitting a publish warning above the cap", and D-152 has just given both numbers a per-tenant carrier. Neither has anything to put in the report. The consequence for the caps is total rather than cosmetic: with no advisory code there is no way to *report* them at all, so the SHOULD was unbuildable and was built as nothing — an NFR ratified 2026-07-28, allocated to the publish engine in §1.2, with no mechanism anywhere. And the consequence for the channel is that a `warnings[]` entry would be a sentence, on the one channel whose whole purpose is that a client can act on it without parsing prose.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — an advisory finding carries a **declared code** exactly as a blocking violation does, in the same catalogue, and never a violation's code reused: the two arrays differ in whether publish proceeds, not in whether the finding is nameable. Two codes, both **advisory** and neither ever blocking. **`TIER_BAND_PRICE_INCREASE`** — a band whose effective unit price exceeds its predecessor's; the rule, its geometry-over-authoring-order clause and its free-opening-band carve-out are `inst-tb-order`'s and none of them moves. **`PLAN_SIZE_SOFT_CAP_EXCEEDED`** (naming the cap, the limit and the count) — a plan above the per-tenant tier-band-per-row or price-row-per-plan soft cap (`pricing_policy_object`, D-152; the ratified 100/500 defaults for a tenant with no entry). One code for the two caps, by D-146's line: the operator's next action is the same shape — shorten the ladder, or split the plan — and the report says which cap and by how much. The caps stay **soft** and the interval caps stay **hard**: `customEveryNDays`/`customEveryNMonths` are enforced by `INVALID_CUSTOM_INTERVAL` and are not part of this rule, which §1.2's allocation row had blurred by listing all four under one "publish-time size validation". Rejected: (a) two codes for the two caps, which discriminates where the remedy does not differ; (b) making the caps blocking so they can reuse a violation code, which contradicts a ratified `SHOULD` and would fail a publish on a threshold a tenant may raise; (c) leaving advisories code-less and letting each consumer match on the message, which makes the report's advisory half unversionable and unlocalizable, and is exactly the arrangement §3.3's status-rendering rule rejects for violations ("the code string is the discriminator a consumer matches on"). Not flagged for veto — no cap value moves, nothing becomes blocking, and the `SHOULD` gains the only thing it was missing.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): the soft-cap advisory rule, which G5 implemented as nothing and reported as a gap rather than minting a code. `TIER_BAND_PRICE_INCREASE` is the other direction — the crate has been raising it since the Slice-3 row-shape rules landed, under a token no document declared; this entry declares the token the implementation had to invent, and the two now agree by statement rather than by coincidence.
- **Propagated**: S1 §1.2 (the NFR allocation row) + §3.3 (the two codes + the advisory-channel rule); S3 §3 `inst-tb-order`; PRD §7.1 `nfr-size-limits`.

#### D-161 [H] The catalog-side snapshot stamp is what the commit can produce — the evaluation-policy segment has no producer

- **Where**: [`design/01-foundation.md`](./design/01-foundation.md) §4.2 (step 4) + §4.4 (the `pricingSnapshotRef` paragraph); [PRD](./PRD.md) §6.7 `fr-pricing-snapshot`.
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-03 while building G5's event payload): `pricingSnapshotRef`'s catalog-side stamp has **three** parts — the pending `CatalogVersion` ref, the resolved price ids, and an **evaluation-policy version** — and the commit can produce two. **No document anywhere names what produces the third or what shape it has**: not §4.2, not §4.4, not `fr-pricing-snapshot`, not the PRD glossary, and not Rating's own composition contract, which merely assigns the segment to this gear ("pricing pre-stamps its **two** segments at publish", `rating design/01` §4.3, `design/11` §4). Rating's `SnapshotComposer` "rejects (fail-closed) a context whose pricing pre-stamp is missing or torn; it never fabricates a segment" — so this is not a documentation nicety. **With no producer, either pricing invents a value or no charge rates**, and the second is what the contract says happens.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — two clauses, and the second is a deliberate non-decision. **(1) Publish stamps the two segments it can produce and stamps *no placeholder* for the third.** A value in a published payload is a value a consumer pins against, immutably and for the retention horizon; the first thing to pin against an invented policy version is the last thing that could ever tell it was invented, and Rating's fail-closed composer is a **correct** guard that a placeholder would defeat by satisfying. An absent segment fails loudly at the boundary that owns the check; a fabricated one fails silently in replay, years later, on posted money. **(2) What the segment *is* — its producer, its format and whether it is a segment at all — is an open fork for the product owner and Rating jointly (§F.1), not a call this wave takes.** The three readings are irreconcilable by inspection and the documents support each: a **vocabulary generation** (which evaluation-policy field set the frozen row content is to be read under — a declared constant, bumped by decision, which is what makes "version" the right word); a **content digest** over the publish's frozen evaluation-policy fields (per-publish, tear-detectable, and arguably a second name for what `catalogVersion` + the price ids already identify); or **no segment at all**, the evaluation-policy fields already resolving through the price ids at the pinned version, in which case the contract table loses a row and Rating countersigns. The options and their consequences are in §F.1. Rejected: (a) picking one here — the three differ in what a *catalog semantic change* does to an already-posted period, which is a determinism guarantee with commercial consequences and a counterparty; (b) stamping a constant `"1"` as a placeholder for the vocabulary reading, which is option one decided by default and by the weakest possible act, a literal in a payload; (c) recording nothing and letting the next group meet it — the fork is a **launch blocker** for rating, and a blocker discovered by a group that cannot decide it is a blocker discovered twice. Not flagged for veto as a decision — clause (1) is the technical call that keeps the fork open safely — but the fork itself is the owner's, jointly with Rating, and it is the wave's one item that cannot be closed here.
- **Propagated**: S1 §4.2 (step 4) + §4.4 (the `pricingSnapshotRef` paragraph); PRD §6.7 `fr-pricing-snapshot`; §F.1 (the fork). **Clause (2) is closed by D-162** (2026-08-03, the owner's call on option (a)); clause (1) — no placeholder, ever — stands unchanged and is what D-162's generation is built to respect.

#### D-162 [H] The evaluation-policy segment is a declared vocabulary generation, and its bump is a replayed log rather than a request

- **Where**: [`design/01-foundation.md`](./design/01-foundation.md) §4.4 (the `pricingSnapshotRef` paragraph + the new normative *evaluation-policy generation* block) + §4.2 (step 4); [`design/03-price-structure.md`](./design/03-price-structure.md) §6 (the `pricing_price` column-list preamble); [PRD](./PRD.md) §1.4 (Price row, `pricingSnapshotRef`) + §6.7 `fr-pricing-snapshot`.
- **Problem** (the §F.1 fork D-161 opened, put to the product owner 2026-08-03 and answered the same day). Three documents require publish to stamp an *evaluation-policy version* and none named a producer or a format, so the commit stamped two segments of three and Rating's fail-closed `SnapshotComposer` rated nothing. A second fact turned up while writing the answer and is the reason the entry is [H] rather than [M]: **the design set never wrote down the evaluation-policy field set at all.** The phrase appears sixty-odd times as an unexpanded token and is expanded **once**, in the PRD §1.4 glossary, as three fields — a list D-40, D-44 and D-45 have each added to since, so the set's only enumeration of the thing was wrong. A generation naming an unwritten set is a constant naming nothing, which is why this entry declares the roster before it declares the version that names it.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — the segment is a **vocabulary generation**, option (a) of §F.1, taken by the product owner. Six clauses, and the fifth is the one that makes the rest worth anything. **(1) Format and meaning.** `ep-<n>`, `n` a positive integer, monotone, **opaque to consumers except for equality**: two snapshots carrying the same generation were frozen under the same evaluation-policy field set, two carrying different generations were not, and nothing else is promised. It is a **declared constant of the gear**, not a per-publish value — every publish of one generation stamps the same string. Current value **`ep-1`**. **(2) The roster.** The evaluation-policy field set is the `pricing_price` fields telling an evaluator **how to derive the billable quantity and select the rate**, which is nine: `model_kind`, `package_size`, `billing_granularity`, `tier_aggregation_window`, `tier_qualification_window`, `aggregation_function`, `aggregation_granularity`, `max_hold_granules`, `included_allowance`. Deliberately outside it, and named there with the reason, are the row's **identity** (`charge_kind`, `meter`, `dimension_key`), its **money** (`amount_minor`, `bands`, `package_price_minor` — D-122's own "legitimate price lever") and the fields that say where a quantity *comes from* rather than how it is derived (`quantity_source`, `manual_quantity`, which S3 §1.8 and S3 §5's misplacement response already treat as adjacent to evaluation policy rather than inside it — both name "an evaluation-policy **or** quantity field", two things and not one). The Slice-6 consumer-contract fields (proration, anchoring, billing timing, tax, rounding) are outside by the same line and move under their own contracts; `rounding_policy_ref` in particular is listed *beside* the evaluation-policy columns in every §6 list that names both. One spelling, `snake_case`, because the set names these fields in three registers and a guard cannot match prose. **(3) The bump rule, stated so a reader can decide a case.** A bump is **required** when a change adds a field to the roster, removes one, or moves a field across the boundary in either direction. A bump is **not** required when a rostered field's requiredness, enum value set, default or meaning changes, when a non-roster field is added to `pricing_price`, or when money, identity or a Slice-6 contract field moves. **(4) What the generation does not claim**, written down rather than left to be discovered, because a reader is entitled to know which risk they carry: it tracks the field **set**, not the meaning of a field. D-58, which made `tier_aggregation_window` mandatory on `package` rows, would not bump it; a decision redefining `peak` would not bump it. That is exactly the coverage option (b) would have bought and this does not. **(5) The bump is a mechanism, not a request.** §4.4 carries a normative machine-readable block: the generation, an **append-only log** of `ep-<n>  <decision-id>  ± <field>` lines, and the out-of-roster set. The roster is **what replaying the log produces**; the generations run without gaps; the last is the declared generation. The gear's build reads that block. A field cannot enter the roster without a log line, a log line **is** a generation, and the last generation is the constant — so there is no edit that adds a field and leaves the version where it was. Rewriting an existing line to backdate a field into a past generation remains possible and is deliberately not defended against: it falsifies what a numbered decision admitted, in a normative document, which is a different act with a different reviewer. **(6) `ep-1` claims no retroactive history.** D-40, D-44, D-45 and D-122 each moved this set before anyone wrote it down, and minting `ep-1..ep-4` for them would produce versions no snapshot was ever stamped with — the same fabrication clause (1) of D-161 refuses, one artifact over. Rejected: **(b) a content digest** over the frozen evaluation-policy fields, which catches semantic change too — the coverage clause (4) admits this decision lacks — and was not taken because it moves on cosmetics (a field re-ordered, a default written out explicitly, a re-publish of identical content under a new price id) and so tells a replay that the semantics changed on the majority of publishes where they did not, while duplicating what `catalogVersion` + the resolved price ids already identify; **(c) no segment at all**, on the ground that the fields resolve through the price ids at the pinned version, which is true of the *values* and not of the *reading* — and which needs Rating to countersign the loss of a contract-table row in two of its own documents, a counterparty transaction to buy the removal of the only field that could ever say a replay is reading yesterday's rows under today's semantics; **(d) declaring the constant without the log**, i.e. option (a) as §F.1 described it — a constant "bumped by decision" is a constant nobody bumps, and one that asserts on posted money a stability that is not there is worse than no constant at all. Not flagged for veto — the fork itself was the owner's call and has been taken; the format, the roster boundary and the guard are technical.
- **Owed to Rating** (the fork was joint): the composer reads the third segment and compares it **for equality only** — an unrecognised generation is a fail-closed refusal, never a value to interpret, and never a reason to fall back to a default reading. Rating's `design/01` §4.3 and `design/11` §4 say pricing pre-stamps "its **two** segments"; that count and the composer's contract are theirs to correct. Until they do, the fail-closed arm is still load-bearing for a *torn* stamp and no longer for an absent one.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`, recorded because it is a real hole and not a rounding of one): the roster is the **price row's**. D-113's `usage_counter_on_plan_change` is a plan-scoped evaluation input that Rating and Subscriptions both read from the pinned snapshot, and it is outside this roster only because the plan-content type it would be classified against does not exist in the crate yet. When the plan-subject content lands it **joins this roster under a bump** — the generation qualifies the snapshot's evaluation semantics and not one table's — and the guard's exhaustive classification extends to that type. A slice that lands it and leaves the roster alone leaves the generation claiming more than it covers.
- **Propagated**: S1 §4.4 (the paragraph + the normative generation block) + §4.2 (step 4); S3 §6 (the column-list preamble, which is where an author adding a column meets the bump rule); PRD §1.4 (both glossary rows) + §6.7 `fr-pricing-snapshot`; §F.1 (the fork, closed).

#### D-163 [H] A version's subject set is closed by its first committed ref — batch atomicity is the registry's contract, not this gear's assumption

- **Where**: [`design/01-foundation.md`](./design/01-foundation.md) §3.6 (`seq-author-publish-fnd`) + §3.7 (`pricing_catalog_version_ref`) + §4.4 (the pin-eligibility paragraph + the new completeness paragraph).
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-03 while building the read side): §4.4's pin-eligibility requires "**every** subject row that version projects" to be warm, which is decidable only if a version's subject set is **closed** — if it can still grow, the predicate counts a set while it is being added to, and a subject arriving after the count is one pin resolving two contents over time, the exact divergence D-101 and D-114 exist to close. What closes it is that **a batch commits atomically into one version**: §3.6 says the registry "batches approved publishes and emits `CatalogVersionPublished`", which read literally is one batch, one version, one event — and **no document in the set says it**. The gap is not academic in either direction. The premise is a property of a *different gear*, so it is exactly the kind of thing a registry change deletes without telling this one; and this gear can break it by itself, which nobody had looked at, because the projector learns each ref's version by asking the registry **per ref** and its knowledge of the set is only as good as the pass that gathered it. Two ordinary conditions leave a pass with a partial set: a **bounded pending scan** whose page splits one batch across passes, and a **per-ref registry error** whose sibling completes the version alone. In both, the version reads complete, the frontier advances, and the straggler then arrives at an already-pinnable version. A second reading of the same silence had already surfaced one section over: a partial `UNIQUE (tenant_id, catalog_version)` on the ref table — defended as making the mapping "a bijection per tenant" — refuses the **normal** case under batching, since a version bundles many refs, and the second finalize into one version fails outright.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — three clauses, and the first is the one that has an owner outside this gear. **(1) The property is normative and it is the registry's.** A batch commits atomically into one version; a version's subject set is therefore **closed the moment any one of its refs commits**, and the only open question about that version is warmth. It is stated in §3.6 beside the sentence it reads out of, because a premise that lives only in the consuming gear's code is deleted by the first change to the producing gear that nobody thought to check — and the registry is **vendored** (D-47, no external counterparty), so there is a document to write it in and a team to owe it. Its storage corollary is stated with it: the mapping version → ref is **one-to-many**, so the index over `(tenant_id, catalog_version)` is the projector's every-ref-of-a-version read and the frontier walk's next-version read, and is deliberately **not unique**. **(2) The projector may decide a version complete only from a pass that could have seen the whole set.** Two conditions say it could not — the pending scan reached its bound, or a ref of the same tenant failed to resolve — and in either the pass resolves and warms as usual and decides **no** completion. The frontier lags rather than over-advances, which is the safe direction and is not silent: `pricing.readmodel.pin_eligibility_overdue` (D-166) fires on exactly that state. The pending scan is bounded **per tenant** for the same reason, since on a cross-tenant page one tenant's stuck backlog would defer every other tenant's completions; how many tenants a pass takes and in what order needs a value and is carried in §F.2. **(3) A ref resolving into a version at or below the frontier is refused, and the price of that refusal belongs here beside the guarantee.** Under (1) and (2) it is unreachable on a correct path — the frontier standing at `V` asserts every version at or below `V` is complete — so it **detects** a broken contract instead of predicting one, which is what keeps a violation loud instead of producing a silently wrong frozen version. What it costs when it fires is total for that publish: the publish becomes **unresolvable at any version**, its subject is never projected, its ref never finalizes, both Critical alarms fire every pass, and nothing self-heals it — the remedy is out of band, §3.6's own "a registry re-request, never a silent re-emit". Clause (2) is what keeps that price attached to a contract violation rather than to an ordinary per-ref outage, which is where the guard as first built could reach it. **No wire code**: the projector has no caller, so a refusal on it has nobody to report to — D-146's own line about this frontier. Rejected: **(a) leaving it a pricing-side assumption with the detection alone**, which leaves a normative rule in §4.4 undecidable by its own text and leaves the gear that can break the premise unable to read it anywhere — the D-01 class across a gear boundary; **(b) dropping the detection once the property is normative**, since a property stated across a gear boundary is a promise and not an enforcement, and the failure it guards is money-visible; **(c) blocking the frontier while **any** ref of the tenant is pending, which is sound and unusable — under a steady publish rate with a p95 ≤ 60s batching delay there is nearly always something pending, so the frontier would sit permanently outside the ≤ 5s pin-lag rule; **(d) keeping the unique index** and giving each ref its own version, which was never available (the version is the registry's, one per batch), or finalizing one ref per version, which leaves the rest of the batch pending forever. Not flagged for veto — nothing consumer-visible moves, and the clause with commercial weight is the price in (3), which is stated rather than chosen: the alternative to paying it is a wrong frozen version.
- **Owed adoption — the registry gear** (`gears/bss/products`, docs-only today, vendored per D-47): the atomicity of a batch stated as a property of `CatalogVersionPublished` on its side too, and the commit instant carried with the committed version, which is the accurate form of the clock D-166 has to approximate locally.
- **Propagated**: S1 §3.6 (the batching sentence + the alarm qualification); §3.7 (`pricing_catalog_version_ref` — the non-unique version index); §4.4 (the pin-eligibility paragraph + the completeness/refusal paragraph).

#### D-164 [H] Pin-eligibility is this tenant's, and the frontier walks forward (D-101/D-114/D-136 amendment)

- **Where**: [`design/01-foundation.md`](./design/01-foundation.md) §1.2 (`fr-consumer-readmodel-resolution`) + §3.7 (`pricing_pin_frontier`) + §4.4 (the pin-eligibility paragraph + the frontier paragraph); [PRD](./PRD.md) `fr-consumer-readmodel-resolution` + §9.2 Tariffs "Compatibility".
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-03 while building the read side): two readings of D-136's advance sentence are unbuildable, and both fail the same way — the frontier stops, forever. **Scope.** D-114 requires "every earlier version to be itself pin-eligible" and D-101 "every subject row that version projects" to be warm, while `CatalogVersion` is minted by a **cross-tenant** registry: a tenant's committed versions are a sparse subset of the global sequence. Read globally, no tenant can ever advance past a version another tenant's publish consumed, so every frontier in a deployment sticks at the first gap; and one tenant's unwarm subject would hold another tenant's pin, across a boundary neither can see. Nothing in the set says which reading is meant. **Reach.** D-136 advances the frontier "in the same transaction that sets the last outstanding `warm_completed` marker of the frontier's **next** version in order". §4.4's own D-114 worked example is `V5` degraded and `V6` fully warm: when `V5` finally completes the frontier moves to `V5`, and `V6` — already complete — never sees another completion to advance on. The frontier stands at `V5` with a complete `V6` behind it, permanently, and the ≤ 5s pin-lag rule is violated for as long as the tenant publishes nothing else.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — one call about what D-136's advance sentence means, two clauses. **(1) Both quantifiers are this tenant's.** The prefix is over **this tenant's committed refs in version order** and the subject set is **this tenant's subjects of that version**. That is what `pricing_pin_frontier` being keyed `tenant_id` **alone** already assumed and what no sentence had said, and it is the only reading under which the frontier is a frontier: a global reading makes pin-eligibility a property no tenant can establish about its own catalog. **(2) The advance walks forward.** After an advance the projector moves the watermark through every immediately-following version of this tenant that is **already** complete. Every step is still "the frontier's next version in order" and still complete, so it is strictly forward and never past a gap — the D-114 prefix is unchanged and still holds by construction; what the walk is not is the literal sentence, which is why it is written down rather than left as an implementation liberty. Rejected: **(a)** the global reading, which is not a stricter rule but an unsatisfiable one; **(b)** re-deriving the prefix at read time, which is precisely the recursive scan D-136 exists to remove; **(c)** leaving the walk to whoever implements the advance, on the grounds that "only ever forward" permits it — a rule that permits two behaviours and requires neither is how one deployment's pin lags a version behind another's with both conforming. Not flagged for veto — this states the only buildable reading of rules already taken.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): nothing on the frontier itself, which is built this way. One contradiction is recorded rather than rounded off: the repository-to-domain mapping renders a refused advance as a **lifecycle refusal**, while D-146 says of this exact case that a regression there is "an internal fault, not a lifecycle refusal, and a wire code for it would document an API a client cannot provoke". Nothing on a wire path can reach either rendering today — the only producer is a background job whose errors are logged — so the mapping is left as it stands and the contradiction is filed here, to be settled by whichever group first gives that variant a caller — which is named 2026-08-03, the implementation having looked: it is the group that lands a **read-model resolution surface**, that being the first thing that puts a client-reachable caller on the frontier at all.
- **Propagated**: S1 §1.2 (`fr-consumer-readmodel-resolution`) + §3.7 (`pricing_pin_frontier` — the walk and the per-tenant scope) + §4.4 (the pin-eligibility paragraph + the frontier paragraph); PRD `fr-consumer-readmodel-resolution` + §9.2 Tariffs "Compatibility"; the D-136 entry carries the amendment pointer.

#### D-165 [H] A version freezes the revision **and** the lifecycle state its own publish judged (D-128 amendment; D-157 extension)

- **Where**: [`design/01-foundation.md`](./design/01-foundation.md) §3.7 (`pricing_catalog_version_ref`) + §4.4 (the projection-source paragraph).
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-03 while building the read side): §4.4 sources the projection from the plan's **current** revision, which is whatever is current when the sweep arrives. Between a publish and its warm sits the registry batching delay, which D-47 budgets at a **maximum of five minutes** — not the 5s warm SLO the earlier defence appealed to — and that window is enough for a second publish. Revision 3 publishes into `V5`, revision 4 publishes into `V6` before `V5` warms, both resolve in one pass: `V5`'s delta freezes revision 4's content. The damage is permanent by construction — delta rows are INSERT-only on the ≥ 7-year horizon, in a store whose entire contract is that a completed version never changes. §4.4 defends the re-drive against **draft** leakage and says nothing at all about a later **published** revision. The same fact one column over moves faster and was missed with it: pinning the revision freezes the row's *content* (a published revision and its revision-scoped children are physically immutable) but **not** its `lifecycle_state`, which still moves — `published → superseded` at the next revision's commit, `published → retired` at retirement. Sellability predicate (4) reads that state from the pin (D-128), so a version whose plan really was sellable projects as unsellable, or projects a third value D-128 never sanctioned for a projected subject.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — the ref row carries the content coordinates its own publish judged, and the projector reads **those**: `subject_revision` and `subject_lifecycle_state` on `pricing_catalog_version_ref`, written by the commit that requested the handle. Both are nullable, because only a revisioned subject kind has them, and `subject_lifecycle_state` admits exactly the two tokens D-128 sanctions for a projected subject (`published`, `retired`) — so a `superseded` state is not expressible in a version at all. A `plan` subject arriving without either value is **refused**, not defaulted: a default is a guess about which content a frozen version froze. Every unit that opens a publish can supply both after its own flip — the publish commit knows the published revision it just flipped, retirement knows its own, a D-99 window mutation knows the revision it read — so the freeze needs no new mechanism, only the two columns. **This is D-157's shape rather than an exception to it:** D-86/D-91 key `pricing_read_model`, which is untouched, and a revision is a property of the **publish unit**, exactly as D-157's subject columns are. **D-128's substance is untouched and is what makes reading a pinned revision safe at all:** `(plan_id, revision)` is the same row whether it stands `published` or `retired`, and retirement is a publish unit of its own that pins its own revision with `retired` and re-projects it under its **own** version — so the state arrives carrying its own version instead of leaking backwards into an older one, and a version published before the retirement keeps reading `published`, which at that version is the truth. Rejected: **(a)** keeping the current-revision read, the pre-fix behaviour and the whole defect; **(b)** defaulting an absent state to `published`, which converts a missing fact into a sellability answer on money; **(c)** re-projecting older versions when a later state moves, which mutates a completed version and is what the read model's contract forbids outright. Not flagged for veto — a frozen version freezing what its own publish judged is what "frozen" already had to mean.
- **Propagated**: S1 §3.7 (`pricing_catalog_version_ref` — the two columns and the two-token constraint); §4.4 (the projection-source paragraph); the D-128 entry carries the amendment pointer.

#### D-166 [M] Degraded, overdue and stale: the three publish-visibility signals get disjoint, measurable predicates (D-136 completion)

- **Where**: [`design/01-foundation.md`](./design/01-foundation.md) §1.2 (`fr-publish-fanout-atomicity` + the propagation NFR row) + §3.6 (the alarm sentence) + §3.7 (`pricing_catalog_version_ref`, `pricing_pin_frontier`) + §4.4 (the degraded paragraph + the new alarm paragraph); [PRD](./PRD.md) `fr-publish-fanout-atomicity`.
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-03 while building the read side): the set names three signals for a publish that has not become visible, and two of them could not be evaluated as written. **The degraded mark has no home.** §4.4 says completion "clears the degraded mark" and **no table in §3.7 has a column for one** — `pricing_read_model` has none and `pricing_operator_flag` is forbidden from carrying version state by D-85. **The 5s SLO has no start instant.** `fr-publish-fanout-atomicity` puts `PlanPublishDegraded` after the commit and explicitly puts the pre-commit batching wait outside it — but the only instants this gear records are `requested_at`, stamped when the handle was asked for, and `committed_at`, stamped by the **finalize**, which never runs on the path where the warm is failing. Measured from `requested_at` the 5s rule marks **every** publish degraded, including every one behaving exactly as D-47 budgets, so the one clause distinguishing a fault from a budgeted wait was unbuildable and the two were bound to collapse into each other. **And `pin_eligibility_overdue` had two referents and no predicate.** §4.4 conditions it on "a version that has not become pin-eligible within the max batching-delay SLO"; D-136 and `pricing_pin_frontier` name the frontier's own `advanced_at`. Neither is sufficient alone: a version **every** subject of which fails to project is never committed in storage at all (the finalize and the warm share a transaction), so on the exact path §4.4 calls "a stuck version now holds the frontier" there is no version to age; and a stale `advanced_at` on its own is satisfied by a tenant that has simply not published, and by every tenant with no frontier row at all, so the alarm degenerates into "this tenant exists" and fires on healthy publishes for the whole of the batching budget. `commit_overdue` alone was stated precisely — "a ref still `pending` past the max batching-delay SLO", the ref's age and nothing else — and is the one signal of the three that needed no repair.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — one instant, and three predicates that no longer overlap. **(1) The ref row records `commit_observed_at`** (§3.7): the instant this gear first saw the registry answer for that ref, written **independently of whether the projection then lands**. That is the missing start for every post-commit clause in the set, and it is what makes `fr-publish-fanout-atomicity`'s own separation of the batching wait from degraded handling measurable instead of aspirational. It is an **upper bound** — the observation lags the registry's commit by at most one sweep pass, so the signal is late rather than false — and the accurate form is the registry supplying its commit instant with the version, which is the owed cross-gear half of D-163's adoption. **(2) `PlanPublishDegraded` keeps the condition the requirement states**: the version has committed and the warm has not landed within the 5s SLO, i.e. unresolved with `commit_observed_at` set and older than the SLO. **(3) The degraded state has no column and needs none.** It is that derived predicate; completion ends it by making the predicate false, which is exactly what §4.4's "no new event name is introduced — consumers observe completion" already means. The projector writes a subject's delta **warm in the same transaction that finalizes its ref**, so "committed but unwarm" is unreachable in storage — which is the whole reason the observation instant has to be recorded rather than derived from the ref's committed-ness. **(4) `commit_overdue` narrows by exactly that qualification**: a ref pending, **not yet observed committed**, past the max batching-delay SLO — the registry has not answered. Nothing goes silent: a ref whose commit was observed and whose warm is failing is (2)'s condition, and either state holds the frontier and is reported by (5). **(5) `pin_eligibility_overdue` fires on the frontier's `advanced_at` in conjunction, never alone**: stale **and** something of this tenant genuinely short of pin-eligibility — either a ref of its own past the SLO, or a committed version standing above its frontier. Both arms are needed, for the two reasons in the problem statement, and finding a stale frontier needs a cross-tenant read because a per-tenant read cannot find a tenant whose frontier is stale precisely because nothing of it has moved. Rejected: **(a)** measuring the 5s from `requested_at`, which marks every healthy publish degraded and is how a Critical becomes noise; **(b)** widening `PlanPublishDegraded` to cover the batching wait so no new instant is needed — the cheapest fix, and it deletes the one sentence in `fr-publish-fanout-atomicity` that tells an operator whether anything is wrong, leaving the state the requirement actually exists for with no name; **(c)** storing a degraded flag, which D-85 forbids for version state and which then has to be cleared by something, where a predicate simply stops being true. Not flagged for veto — the requirement is upheld exactly as written and gains the instant it always needed.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): the column and both narrowed predicates. G6 marks degraded on the ref's own age against the batching SLO — the merge clause (2) refuses — precisely because it had no post-commit instant to measure from; with `commit_observed_at` the two signals separate, and the emitted event's dedup key can name the publish by its plan and **version** as originally specified, the version being known at every instant the corrected condition is observable.
- **Propagated**: S1 §1.2 (`fr-publish-fanout-atomicity` + the propagation NFR row) + §3.6 (the alarm sentence) + §3.7 (`pricing_catalog_version_ref` — the instants incl. `commit_observed_at`; `pricing_pin_frontier` — `advanced_at` is a referent, not a predicate) + §4.4 (the degraded paragraph + the alarm paragraph); PRD `fr-publish-fanout-atomicity`.

#### D-167 [M] What a plan-subject delta carries before Slices 4/6/7/10, and what each absence owes (D-121 completion)

- **Where**: [`design/01-foundation.md`](./design/01-foundation.md) §4.4 (the D-121 projected-row-set paragraph + the new payload paragraph); [`design/07-pricewindow-linkage.md`](./design/07-pricewindow-linkage.md) `inst-sg-pinned`; [PRD](./PRD.md) §9.2 Tariffs "Compatibility".
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-03 while building the read side): three silences about the same object, and the first one is that **no document declares the delta's field list at all**. §4.4 says what it must *carry*, §3.7 says how it is *keyed*, §3.1's `ReadModel` line is a sketch — so the first implementation had to invent a schema, exactly the position D-158 found the audit log's discriminators in. **D-121's horizon cannot be evaluated**: it filters on a **window set**, and the `PriceWindow` store is Slice 7's and unbuilt, so no interval columns exist and the predicate has no operand — taken literally the rule projects **nothing**, since no row has a window set and no row intersects `[projection_time − H, ∞)`. **And `inst-sg-pinned` is already false**: it requires all six sellability predicates to be point-in-time evaluable from the pinned read model, while the facts three of them read are owned by slices that do not exist — the window intervals and derived coverage end (Slice 7), the GA-gate and prepaid-execution flags (Slices 4 and 10), and the registry `sellable` flag (D-46, and therefore the registry gear). A consumer coding against the promise gets a version that answers three, with no way to tell "this predicate is false" from "this version cannot evaluate this predicate".
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — one call about what a version contains today, in three clauses. **(1) The payload is a declared vocabulary.** Wire keys are `camelCase`, one spelling, declared once; §4.4 owns the **envelope** (the subject's identity, the pinned revision and lifecycle state per D-165, and the D-121 row set) and each slice declares the facts it owns into it, as Slice 6 declares its contract pair in its own §6. The point is the same as D-158's: a vocabulary the implementation had to invent is a vocabulary the next document contradicts by accident. **(2) The horizon is not evaluable before Slice 7, the projection proceeds without it, and the premise that makes that safe is stated where the group that deletes it will meet it.** What the bound buys is D-121's own sentence — a delta is O(live keys × windows within `H`), never O(the plan's accumulated history) — and a plan's accumulated history is its **superseded** price rows; `published → superseded` has exactly two sanctioned producers, the **D-88** supersession unit and the **D-100** cutover, and this gear has neither.
