So the set the horizon filters is empty, the delta is the plan's `published` rows capped by the §14 per-plan soft cap, and the bound excludes nothing. **The group that builds either producer deletes this premise and owes the horizon** — and with it `H`'s own input, "the longest billing cycle sold on the plan", which is W6's term and is likewise unavailable here. This is D-155 clause (3)'s shape on the read side, and the same reasoning applies to why it is written down: an unstated "all" is how a later slice removes a guarantee nobody told it about. **(3) `inst-sg-pinned` is a claim about the finished gear, and which predicates a version can answer follows the slices that landed.** A version projected today answers (2) committed-version addressability, (3) `availableFrom`/`availableTo` and (4) the plan lifecycle state; (1), (5) and (6) are not evaluable from it, and each is named with the slice that makes it evaluable. Absences of this kind are declared rather than discovered — and are deliberately distinguished from the one absence that is a **rule**: operator-plane flags are never in a version (D-85), so a later reader completing the payload does not complete it with them. Rejected: **(a)** projecting nothing until Slice 7, which is D-121 read literally and leaves the read side with no output to build against for the length of two slices; **(b)** synthesising the missing facts — a coverage end with no windows, a `sellable` with no registry — which freezes invented values immutably, D-161 clause (1) one store over; **(c)** leaving `inst-sg-pinned` unqualified and letting the first consumer discover the shortfall, which spends a gate's credibility on a promise the set knew was not yet true. Not flagged for veto — nothing decided here changes the target state; it dates it.
- **Propagated**: S1 §4.4 (the D-121 paragraph's premise clause + the payload paragraph); S7 `inst-sg-pinned`; PRD §9.2 Tariffs "Compatibility".

#### D-168 [M] The cross-boundary contract pair is stamped as a pair or not at all — its text has no source

- **Where**: [`design/06-consumer-contracts.md`](./design/06-consumer-contracts.md) §6 (`pricing_read_model` contract-level fields) + §9 (the contract-pair AC); §F.1 (the fork).
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-03 while building the read side): S6 §6 requires **both** `crossBoundaryChangePolicy` and `crossBoundaryWarningText` on every resolved `plan` subject row, and its §9 AC restates it. The policy value is named verbatim (`cancel_plus_new`); the **text** is named in no document of this set — not in S6, not in the PRD, nowhere — while §6 calls both "launch-constant, tenant-wide values", i.e. asserts that a constant exists without declaring it. A projector therefore holds one half of a two-field contract and has nothing to put in the other, and the two available moves are both wrong: publishing the named half alone freezes **half a contract** into an immutable version, and inventing the other half freezes a value no document declares.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — two clauses, and the second is a deliberate non-decision. **(1) The pair is stamped as a pair or not at all.** Until the text has a declared source **neither** field is projected: a version answers this contract with nothing rather than with half of it. This is D-161 clause (1)'s reasoning one artifact over and it bites harder here, because a snapshot segment can at least be absent while a **frozen version can never be corrected** — a placeholder warning is pinned on every version stamped with it for the retention horizon, and the first consumer to render it is the last thing that could ever tell it was invented. **(2) What the text is, and where it comes from, is an open fork for the product owner (§F.1).** The three readings are not reconcilable by inspection: a **declared launch-constant** in S6, which is the shape §6 already asserts and which makes customer-facing copy a normative clause of a design document with no localization story anywhere in this set; a **per-tenant configurable** on `pricing_policy_object` with a declared launch default, the D-152 shape, on a carrier the owner has already marked provisional; or **dropping the field**, publishing the machine-readable marker alone and letting the surface that renders the warning own its own copy — which needs the consuming side to countersign, since §6 and the AC both require the pair. The recommendation is in §F.1, with the argument that decides it. Rejected: **(a)** picking one here — the disagreement is about who owns customer-facing wording and in how many languages, which is not a technical call; **(b)** stamping the policy alone and calling the text optional, which is clause (1) refused; **(c)** recording nothing, on the grounds that no consumer reads a delta yet — the field is required by an AC that is currently unsatisfiable, and an unsatisfiable AC that nobody wrote down passes by being forgotten. Not flagged for veto as a decision — clause (1) is the technical call that keeps the fork open safely; the fork itself is the owner's.
- **Propagated**: S6 §6 (the contract-level fields) + §9 (the AC, restated as both-or-neither); §F.1 (the fork). **Clause (2) is closed by D-169** (2026-08-03, the owner's call on option (c)); clause (1) — never half a contract in a frozen payload — is **discharged rather than overridden**, there being one field left and so no half to publish, and it is the argument that decided the fork.

#### D-169 [M] The cross-boundary contract publishes the marker; the warning copy belongs to the surface that renders it

- **Where**: [`design/06-consumer-contracts.md`](./design/06-consumer-contracts.md) §1.5 (scope, both halves) + §3 (`inst-pi-crossboundary`) + §6 (the contract-level field) + §8 (the proration DoD) + §9 (the AC); [PRD](./PRD.md) AC #66 + §17.6 (the cross-boundary launch-scope note).
- **Problem** (the §F.1 fork D-168 opened, put to the product owner 2026-08-03 and answered the same day). S6 §6 required the K3 marker as a **pair** — `crossBoundaryChangePolicy = cancel_plus_new` beside a `crossBoundaryWarningText` whose value is named in no document of this set — so the pair was unstampable, the §9 AC requiring it was unsatisfiable, and D-168 held the line at both-or-neither: a version answers this contract with nothing rather than with half of it. Three readings were put up: a declared launch constant in S6, a per-tenant configurable on `pricing_policy_object`, or dropping the field. **A fact turned up while writing the answer that the fork statement did not have, and it is why this entry is short: the warning already had an owner in this set.** PRD **AC #66** requires the preview/migration UI to warn that in-place proration credit is forfeited and to take the operator's explicit confirmation, and §17.6's launch-scope note points at AC #66 for exactly that. The catalog field was a **second** home for a sentence this PRD had already placed on the surface that displays it — and the second home is the one with an INSERT-only, ≥ 7-year, never-corrected store behind it.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — option **(c)**, taken by the product owner on the recommendation's own ground. **(1) The catalog publishes the marker and no copy.** `crossBoundaryChangePolicy = cancel_plus_new` stays exactly where D-91's keying put it, on every resolved `plan` subject row of `pricing_read_model`, a launch-constant tenant-wide value; `crossBoundaryWarningText` **leaves the contract**. What is published is machine-readable and derivable by nobody but this gear; what a human is shown is not a catalog fact. **(2) The copy's owner is named, because dropping a field without naming one deletes the obligation rather than moving it.** PRD AC #66 is the normative home — the warning and its confirmation, keyed off the marker — and S6 §1.5 lists the *wording* out of scope beside the other things Subscriptions owns (plan-change execution, entitlement enforcement). This is the second time this boundary has been crossed in this direction and the first is the precedent: **D-93** already moved the boundary *class* from a published stamp to a change-time computation on the consumer's side, so the copy follows a route the classification took first. **(3) D-168 clause (1) is discharged, not overridden**: both-or-neither was a rule about a two-field contract, and with one field there is no half to stamp — so the AC D-168 recorded as unsatisfiable becomes satisfiable, and the read side gains a field it can stamp today rather than a fork it must wait on. Clause (1)'s ban on freezing a value no document declares is untouched and is what decided the fork. Rejected: **(a)** a declared launch constant in S6, the shape §6 already asserted — lost on the **store** rather than the sentence: whatever S6 declares, publish freezes into a delta row that is INSERT-only over the ≥ 7-year truth horizon in a store whose contract is that a completed version never changes, so a typo, a tone change or a second language is permanent for every version already stamped with it, and this design set has **no localization story anywhere**; a customer-visible string frozen in one language forever is an irreversible decision bought for a convenience that is cheaper to obtain at render time. **(b)** a per-tenant configurable on `pricing_policy_object` with a declared launch default, the D-152 shape and the option to take if the copy must stay catalog-owned — lost because re-authorability is **going forward only**: every version already stamped keeps whatever the tenant had configured at the time, so the irreversibility (a) is rejected for is unchanged for everything already published, and it lands customer-facing copy on a carrier D-152's own confirmation marks **provisional** (the owner expects those settings to move to a settings gear that does not exist in this repository). Between them, (a) and (b) buy the catalog nothing a consumer cannot obtain at render time and each spends the one property this store cannot give back. **(c)** *keeping the pair and waiting* — i.e. leaving the fork open — was priced by D-168 itself: the AC stays unsatisfiable and a version answers the cross-boundary contract with nothing, indefinitely, for a field no consumer document has ever named. Not flagged for veto — the fork was the owner's call and has been taken; the surfaces, the discharge of D-168 clause (1) and the countersign below are technical.
- **Owed to Subscriptions** (the countersign this decision buys, and the reason it is a transaction rather than an edit): §6 and its AC **required** the pair, so removing a field from a published consumer contract is a counterparty act — the D-162 shape, where option (c) would have needed Rating to countersign away a contract-table row. What is owed their side is the statement that the **wording is theirs**: their `SEAMS.md` **SUB-P1** already carries the plan-change classification contract and D-93's change-time boundary class, and their `design/03-plan-changes.md` already routes cross-boundary changes to cancel+new through `ChangeClassifier` **without naming any text field** — which is the whole reason this is cheap. The marker is their input; PRD AC #66's warn-and-confirm is rendered by their plan-change preview; nothing in their set says today who authors that sentence, and that is theirs to write. Until they do, the sentence has a named surface and no named author — a state this entry removes from **pricing** and not from the set as a whole.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`, recorded because it is created by this decision rather than inherited): the projector owes the marker. G6 stamps **neither** half — correctly, under D-168 clause (1) — and `pricing/src/domain/projection.rs` lists the pair among the facts a version deliberately omits, `pricing-sdk/src/catalog_version.rs` calling it "the Slice-6 cross-boundary pair" in the same breath. With the text gone, the marker is a tenant-wide constant with a value named verbatim in S6 and nothing left to wait for, so a plan subject's delta owes one field, and both comments name a second one that no longer exists. No wire behaviour moves with it: nothing reads a delta yet (G7 owns the read surface).
- **Propagated**: S6 §1.5 (in-scope "marker only" + the out-of-scope wording) + §3 (`inst-pi-crossboundary`) + §6 (the contract-level field) + §8 (the proration DoD) + §9 (the AC); PRD AC #66 (the normative home of the warning) + §17.6 (the launch-scope note); §F.1 (the fork, closed); the D-168 entry carries the closure pointer.

#### D-170 [M] A plan route addresses the plan's editable revision, and its entity tag names which one

- **Where**: [`design/01-foundation.md`](./design/01-foundation.md) §3.7 (`pricing_plan`) + §4.3; [`design/02-plan-definition.md`](./design/02-plan-definition.md) §5; [`design/03-price-structure.md`](./design/03-price-structure.md) §5; [PRD](./PRD.md) `fr-concurrent-edit`.
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-03 while building G7, the gear's REST surface): two silences that are harmless apart and unsound together. **What an authoring read answers.** S2 §5's `GET /bss-pricing/v1/plans/{planId}` and S3 §5's `GET …/plans/{planId}/prices` both carry the Purpose cell "draft for authors; published via read model" and nothing else, so neither says what the surface returns for the ordinary state of a plan under revision — one that holds a published current revision *and* an open draft. **What a tag contains.** §3.7 gives `pricing_plan` an "ETag/row-version" column and §1.2's `fr-concurrent-edit`, §3.3 and the §5 tables say "ETag" and stop; **no document in this set gives a plan tag a shape**, so the set does not tell a client what to copy back and a client cannot be written against it. Together they are the defect: a plan route resolves to one of **two** revisions and a token carrying only a row version cannot say which one it was read from, while the two counters are unrelated — a freshly opened successor stands at the initial version, exactly where a first draft stands. So a tag minted against revision *N* satisfied the compare-and-swap on *N+1* **with no race window at all**, and G7's own suite drove precisely that shape and called it the ordinary path. That is the lost update **D-145** spends its entry eliminating, arriving through the *revision* instead of the number: D-145 closed it in storage by keeping a consumed number consumed, and a plan route reopens it in transport by addressing a plan without naming a revision.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — one call about what a plan route addresses, in three clauses. **(1) An authoring read serves the truth plane, and it answers for the plan's *editable* revision**: `GET …/plans/{planId}` returns the open draft revision when the plan holds one and the current revision otherwise. "Published via read model" was a statement about where published content is **consumed** and never that the authoring read hides it — an author opening a plan to revise it must see what is current before a draft exists, and the read model answers a different question (a frozen per-`CatalogVersion` projection reached by a pin, §4.4). The price list is the same call one surface over: it returns the plan's `draft` **and** `published` rows and nothing else — `superseded` is history no longer current on its key (Slice 12's subject), and `abandoned` is not a price-row state at all. **(2) The tag names the revision as well as the version.** Rendered `"<revision>-<version>"`, two decimals joined by a hyphen inside the quoted entity-tag string; emitted by the read, by the create's 201 and by every mutating response; required by `PATCH` and `POST …/abandon`, and on the arm that opens a successor required to name the **current** revision — what the caller actually read — before anything is opened. A mismatch in **either** component is `STALE_VERSION` (409, §3.3): the same refusal in substance, the caller working from a read it did not refresh, and what moved on is *which revision the plan offers for editing*. **Nothing is minted.** **(3) The tag is opaque to the caller**, copied back verbatim into `If-Match` (D-171) and never constructed, parsed or compared. The rendering in (2) is stated so the set says what a client receives, not so a client may read it: one that treats the tag as an opaque string is correct under any future rendering, and one that parses it relies on something this contract does not offer. A wildcard, a weak validator, a list or any token that is not this rendering is a **malformed request** under the validation envelope, not a precondition failure. **Price tags are deliberately unqualified and D-141 is untouched**: a price route addresses one row by `priceId`, so tag and row are one to one and there is nothing to disambiguate; the asymmetry between the planes follows the addressing rather than a preference. Rejected: **(a)** leaving the tag a bare row version, which is the pre-fix behaviour and the whole defect; **(b)** **naming the revision in the path** (`…/plans/{planId}/revisions/{revision}`), sound and paid for by every caller — an author would have to learn a revision number before editing the plan they have just read, and it reopens D-140's route shape to carry a fact the tag can carry; **(c)** a **second header** (`If-Match-Revision`), which splits one precondition across two fields that can disagree and hands a caller two things to copy back where the point of an entity tag is that there is one; **(d)** an **opaque encoding** (base64, or a hash of the pair), which buys by construction the opacity clause (3) states as a rule, and costs the readable 409 whose message names both revisions. Not flagged for veto — nothing commercial moves; what is decided is which of two revisions a precondition is tested against, and only one answer is sound.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): nothing. G7 built all three clauses, the tag shape after an independent review found the defect, and the binding has a test per call site — each verified by deleting that call site and watching exactly one test fail.
- **Propagated**: S1 §3.7 (the `pricing_plan` bullet — the tag's shape, its opacity, and why the price tag is not qualified) + §4.3 (the read's answer for a spent plan, with D-172); S2 §5 (the `GET` row's Purpose cell + the route-and-tag paragraph); S3 §5 (the list row's Purpose cell + the authoring-list paragraph + the price-token clause); PRD `fr-concurrent-edit`.
- **Completed by D-176 (2026-08-03)**: this entry gave the tag a shape and left *where the comparison happens* unstated, which is the whole of a swap's strength. On the arm that opens a successor the write is an **insert**, so the comparison sat outside the writing transaction and a concurrent publish could leave the successor copied from a revision the caller never read — this decision's own defect, on its own arm, one window further out.

#### D-171 [M] The preconditions have wire names — `If-Match` and `Idempotency-Key`, both required

- **Where**: [`design/01-foundation.md`](./design/01-foundation.md) §3.3; [PRD](./PRD.md) `fr-concurrent-edit` + `fr-mutation-idempotency`.
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-03 while building G7): every §5 API table in this set carries an **Idempotency** column — nine of the twelve slices have one — and its cells read `ETag`, `client idempotency key`, `idempotency key`, `idempotency key / ETag`, `per revision`, `per decision` or `—`. **Not one document in the set names a request header.** `If-Match` and `Idempotency-Key` appear nowhere in the PRD, in DESIGN, in this register or in any of the twelve slices. So the set names the *concepts* a mutation is guarded by and never their transport: a client generated from this set sends neither header and discovers both as a 400, which is D-140's shape one field over — a documented surface that cannot be called as documented — and D-158's one store over, a vocabulary the implementation had to invent. Six of G7's nine routes require one, and the group had to choose both spellings itself.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — the column's tokens get their wire form, once, in the Foundation section every slice §5 inherits from, as the D-125 pagination contract and the D-140 route shape are. **(1)** An `ETag` cell means a **required `If-Match`** request header carrying the entity tag the addressed resource's own read returned — RFC 9110's precondition field. **(2)** A `client idempotency key` cell, and its variant spellings `idempotency key` and `idempotency key / ETag`, means a **required `Idempotency-Key`** request header whose value is `pricing_idempotency_dedup`'s `client_key` (§3.7). **(3)** A cell naming a **natural** idempotency (`per revision`, `per plan revision`, `per decision`) requires **no header**: the call is idempotent on a key the request already carries, and there is nothing for a caller to mint. **(4)** `—` means the surface is not a mutation. **(5)** Both headers are **required, not advisory**: an absent one is a malformed request under the validation envelope, **no new code**, which is exactly the rule D-141 already stated for the absent precondition and is now stated once for the absent key as well. The spellings are the standards' rather than this gear's — which is the reason a decision can settle them at all. Rejected: **(a)** leaving the column as the declaration on the ground that "ETag" implies `If-Match` to a reader who knows HTTP — it names no *field*, and the idempotency key has no such convention to lean on, so exactly half the mapping would still be invented, by each client separately; **(b)** declaring the headers in each slice's §5 table, nine restatements of one rule and the arrangement D-125 and D-140 both rejected; **(c)** making the idempotency key **optional** where the column names it, which is a different decision about at-most-once and would leave the authoring plane's two creates retryable into duplicate rows. Not flagged for veto — the requirement is the one the set already states and only its spelling is added. **Deliberately not folded into D-170**, though the same act of writing the `OpenAPI` registration found both: that entry decides what a plan tag *contains* and would stand whole if these header names were obvious, and this one decides what carries a precondition on **every** surface of the gear including the slices nobody has built, and would stand whole if a plan tag were a bare version. Two calls, not one seen twice.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): nothing on the declarations, which G7 registered on all six mutating routes through `ParamLocation::Header`. One **test** debt is recorded rather than decided: no test reads the registration's params, so deleting a declaration fails nothing — a guard with no test, which is the shape the group's own review round was about. It is one assertion in the crate's module test and belongs to whichever wave next touches the surface.
- **Propagated**: S1 §3.3 (the new normative statement, beside the pagination contract and the route shape); PRD `fr-concurrent-edit` + `fr-mutation-idempotency`.

#### D-172 [M] Three surfaces owe `PLAN_ABANDONED_NO_SUCCESSOR`, not four — the create names no plan id, and the read answers 404 (D-145 amendment)

- **Where**: [`design/01-foundation.md`](./design/01-foundation.md) §4.3; [`design/02-plan-definition.md`](./design/02-plan-definition.md) §5.
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-03 while building G7): D-145's amendment gave the spent-plan state its refusal and named the surfaces that owe it. The paragraph opens "**Every surface above names a `planId`**" and then enumerates four — `POST /bss-pricing/v1/plans`, `PATCH`, `POST …/publish`, `POST …/abandon` — and **its own opening sentence excludes the first**: `POST /bss-pricing/v1/plans` is the one row on that table that names no `planId`. The mechanism it gives that arm, "retried with that id collides on the `(plan_id, revision)` primary key", presupposes a **caller-supplied** plan id, and §4.3 states the opposite four paragraphs earlier and in as many words — *a plan id is minted server-side*. A retried create carries an `Idempotency-Key` and no plan id at all, and is answered by the replay path (§3.7, D-142), so the collision is real in storage and reachable by no caller. The same enumeration is silent in the other direction: `GET /bss-pricing/v1/plans/{planId}` **does** name a `planId` and is not among the four. One mis-enumeration, wrong at both ends, and it is the one entry of G7's register that is a finding **against the design set** rather than against the code.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — two clauses, and neither disturbs what D-145 decided. **(1) Three authoring surfaces owe the refusal** — `PATCH …/plans/{planId}`, `POST …/plans/{planId}/publish` and `POST …/plans/{planId}/abandon`, each finding no current revision to succeed from or no open draft to act on, each answering `PLAN_ABANDONED_NO_SUCCESSOR` (422). The create arm is **struck**, and with it the primary-key-collision mechanism. **(2) The read answers 404**, and that is not a contradiction of (1): the route serves the plan's *editable* revision (D-170 clause (1)) and there is none, so what is absent is the representation this route offers rather than the plan — whose abandoned revision the Slice-12 history surface still shows. **D-145's substance is untouched and is what the correction rests on**: `revision` is an identity, a spent id stays spent, and the honest refusal is the answer. What falls away with the create arm is **one rejected alternative's argument and not its conclusion** — the amendment refused "minting `max(revision) + 1` on the create path as well" partly because a retried create naming the same id would then silently open a second revision of an existing plan; under server-minted ids a retried create names no id, so that consequence is unreachable and the alternative is not merely rejected but inapplicable. The conclusion stands on the untouched half: exempting revision `0` from the identity rule reintroduces the unstable name the whole rule removes. Rejected: **(a)** **accepting a caller-supplied plan id**, which would make the struck arm reachable as written and is a change to the gear's identity model — the id becomes a client's choice and is then redundant with the idempotency key that already makes a retried create safe; **(b)** **extending the refusal to the read**, which makes a `GET` answer 422 for a resource that exists and tells a caller about a precondition on a call that has none; **(c)** leaving the enumeration standing as harmless prose, on the ground that no code would ever be raised there — it is the arm a reader implements first, and the group that read it found it unbuildable and had to decide the question itself. Not flagged for veto — a refusal no caller could reach is removed and a read's answer is written down; nothing consumer-visible moves.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): **two of the three arms, and the read.** *(Corrected 2026-08-03. This clause read "nothing. G7 raises the refusal on all three surviving arms and answers 404 on the read", which was false when it was written: the third arm's surface is `POST /bss-pricing/v1/plans/{planId}/publish`, and that is **not a mounted route** — the gear registers nine paths and it is not among them, so there is nothing for the refusal to be raised on. The two arms that exist — `PATCH …/plans/{planId}` and `POST …/plans/{planId}/abandon` — and the read's 404 are paid. The publish arm is owed by the group that mounts the route, which is Slice 5's, together with everything else on that path. This is the **second** instance in this program of a register entry overstating its own discharge, and it survived because the group that found it was forbidden to reconcile a divergence by editing a document; it is corrected in the next documentation wave, which is the mechanism working rather than a second failure.)* The reciprocal is worth recording and is unaffected: **D-145's amendment named G7 as owing the code and G7 has paid it** on every surface it mounts, in process and on the wire, so the "this gear has no authoring REST surface yet" sentences in §4.3 and S2 §5 are discharged in the same edit as this decision.
- **Propagated**: S1 §4.3 (the spent-plan paragraph's arm list, its mechanism sentence, the read's answer, and the discharge); S2 §5 (the third-Foundation-refusal paragraph, split into the three arms and the two surfaces that do not owe it); the D-145 entry carries the amendment pointer.

#### D-173 [M] A `PATCH` carries one facet of a plan's shape, and the coherent multi-facet update is a capability nobody has designed

- **Where**: [`design/02-plan-definition.md`](./design/02-plan-definition.md) §5.
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-03 while building G7): S2 §5's `PATCH /bss-pricing/v1/plans/{planId}` named **four** facets in one verb — "Update draft shape (cycle, phases, add-ons, descriptors)" — and the storage this set specifies cannot apply them as one. The cycle lives on the plan revision row; phases, add-on rules and descriptors live in three revision-scoped child tables (D-83/D-106); and **all four** are versioned against the revision's *single* row version and each advances it. So a request carrying two facets matches the caller's `If-Match` on the first and cannot match it on the second, and applying them in sequence leaves a **visibly half-applied revision** between two transactions — the state §4.2's commit refuses on the publish plane for exactly the same reason, that a reader in between sees content no rule judged.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — **(1)** a `PATCH` on this surface carries **exactly one** of the four facets; more than one is a malformed request under the validation envelope (400, **no new code**), and the response names the facets presented. An author changing four facets makes four calls, each with the tag the previous one returned (D-170). **(2) The capability the Purpose cell promised is named rather than dropped**: a coherent multi-facet update is *one operation applying every presented facet inside one transaction against one tag, advancing it once*, and this set has designed neither its request shape nor its partial-failure report nor its interaction with the D-141 per-row tags a price-bearing variant would need. It is owed by whichever wave next changes the draft patch shape, and it is owed to a **design decision**, not to a test suite. Rejected: **(a)** **applying the facets in sequence**, the reading the cell invites — it cannot honour the caller's precondition past the first facet and leaves the half-applied state above; **(b)** **versioning the four facets separately**, one tag per child table, a storage change that contradicts D-83's copy-on-new-revision model (a child row's version would have to survive a revision copy) and hands an author four tags for one revision; **(c)** **taking the first facet and ignoring the rest**, which produces a plan whose author believes it holds a change it does not. Not flagged for veto: no requirement of this set asks for a combined update — `fr-plan-versioning` and the S2 DoDs govern *what* a revision may contain and never how many facets one call may carry — so what is narrowed is a table cell's description of a verb, not a promise made to an operator.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): nothing on the rule, which G7 built and argued in the request type's own doc. Clause (2)'s operation is owed to a future wave, and the register is where it is recorded so that wave does not have to rediscover it from the code.
- **Propagated**: S2 §5 (the `PATCH` row's Purpose cell + the one-facet rule paragraph).

#### D-174 [M] The idempotency digest is over the request this gear models, not over the bytes it arrived in

- **Where**: [`design/01-foundation.md`](./design/01-foundation.md) §3.7 (`pricing_idempotency_dedup`); [PRD](./PRD.md) `fr-mutation-idempotency`.
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-03 while building G7): §3.7 gives the dedup row "a request-payload digest", and the whole of D-142 and D-143 turns on comparing it — a match replays the stored response, a mismatch is `IDEMPOTENCY_PAYLOAD_MISMATCH` (409), and expiry is evaluated before the comparison. **Nothing says what the digest is taken over**, and the two readings differ on the wire. Over the **received bytes**, a retry that merely re-serializes the same request — a different client, a re-encoding proxy, any library whose member order is not stable — digests differently and is refused `IDEMPOTENCY_PAYLOAD_MISMATCH`: a 409 the caller cannot fix, on the one call `fr-mutation-idempotency` obliges them to retry, for a payload that is the same request. Over the **parsed request**, member order and whitespace stop deciding a replay, and a second consequence follows that has to be stated rather than found: a retry that adds a member this gear does not model digests identically to one without it, and therefore replays.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — the digest is taken over a **canonical rendering of the request as this gear models it**: the parsed request re-serialized with a deterministic member order, so that two encodings of one request digest alike and `IDEMPOTENCY_PAYLOAD_MISMATCH` means the caller changed the request rather than the encoder. Two consequences are normative rather than incidental. **(1) A member the gear does not model is outside the digest**, so a retry that adds one is a replay — the correct answer for a field the gear ignores, and the same fact as not refusing a legitimate re-encoding. **(2) Every map-valued member of a request type has a determinate order**; the rendering is canonical only if it has, and this set carries one such member already, the D-48 descriptor set's additional properties. Rejected: **(a)** the **byte digest**, which turns a correct retry into an unfixable 409 and makes at-most-once depend on a client's serializer; **(b)** the byte digest plus a "retry the identical bytes" obligation on callers, a contract no gateway or SDK in the path can be held to; **(c)** **closing the request types** to unmodelled members, which fixes (1) by rejecting them at parse — a real option, and a **different** decision with a cost this set accepts nowhere else: it breaks every additive client-side rollout, and §3.3's read-model contract takes the opposite posture in as many words ("additive-only within a major version"). Not flagged for veto — the digest is internal to the gear and never crosses the wire; what is decided is which of two implementations of one unfinished sentence a client experiences, and only one of them is survivable by a retry.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): nothing. G7 built the parsed rendering and both consequences, and states them in the digest function's own doc.
- **Propagated**: S1 §3.7 (the `pricing_idempotency_dedup` bullet — the digest's subject and its two consequences); PRD `fr-mutation-idempotency`.

#### D-175 [M] The `action` vocabulary is closed by the mutations this set specifies, not by its opening list — `create`, `update`, `delete` (D-158 amendment)

- **Where**: [`design/05-governance.md`](./design/05-governance.md) §6 (`pricing_audit_log` writer contract); [`design/01-foundation.md`](./design/01-foundation.md) §3.7 (`pricing_audit_log`); [PRD](./PRD.md) `fr-audit-completeness`.
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-03 while closing the implementation's debt against this set): S5 §6 declares the `action` vocabulary and opens it with "exactly the records this design set already requires somebody to write" — `publish`, `abandon`, `retire`, `approve`, `reject`, `deny`, `backdate_import`, `policy_update`. Of the **six** mutating authoring surfaces this set specifies, that list names **one** (`abandon`, by D-145). The same section requires more than it enumerates, twice and normatively: §1.2's "every mutation leaves an immutable trail an auditor can rely on for 7+ years", and §8's `dod-audit` — "**every mutation MUST record** actor/timestamp/before-after/approval trail". Draft authoring is a mutation. So five of six mutations were obliged to write a record and had **no token to write it under**, and an implementer reading the opening enumeration as the closed set would write one record of six — the state the code debt register classes *wrong now* rather than *absent*, on the ground that a silently incomplete trail is worse than a visibly missing one: `pricing_audit_log` is the store D-12 confines to the Auditor, and it answered "who changed this plan" with nothing, indistinguishably from "nobody did". The implementation wrote all six records and **minted the three verbs itself**, reporting them as minted in four places rather than treating them as declared, because D-158's own instruction is that "a slice that adds an audited record adds its token **here**" — this document — and the group's constraints forbade it editing one.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — three clauses, and the middle one is the reason this is an entry rather than an append. **(1) The three draft-authoring verbs are declared**: `create` (a draft plan revision minted — a plan's first draft at revision `0` **and** the successor revision a `PATCH` on a published plan opens, D-170 — and a draft price row authored), `update` (a plan facet replaced, one per call by D-173; a draft price row's content replaced), `delete` (a never-published draft price row removed, which `inst-ps-nodelete` keeps off a published one). `abandon` stays **distinct** from `delete` by D-145: the plan revision row survives as a tombstone and its number stays consumed, so a reader who cannot tell the two apart cannot tell a discarded draft from a row that was never there. **(2) The set's closure rule is written down, as the companion of D-158's**: D-158 says a token with **no writer is not declared**; the converse now holds too — **no writer without a token**. Every mutating surface this design set specifies carries an `action` here, and the roster is audited against that set rather than appended to as records happen to land. This is the clause that would have caught the gap: the opening list stated the vocabulary's **provenance** and was read as its **closure**, which is a distinction no sentence had drawn. **(3) `subject_kind` gained nothing, and that is checked rather than assumed**: `price_unit` is already in the enumeration D-158 takes verbatim from `pricing_approval`, so a price row's record needed no new token — recorded because the implementation looked and the next reader will wonder. The `denied attempt` record correspondingly stays **undeclared**, D-158's first rule working in the other direction: this gear has no writer for it (no approval record, no approval `chain_id`), and a token declared here with no writer reads as coverage to everyone who greps for it. Rejected: **(a)** writing only `abandon` and leaving five of six surfaces silent, which leaves a normative MUST unbuilt over a naming question and is the larger fault; **(b)** treating the three as a wire code and refusing to spell them at all, which is the act that would have needed the owner — an `action` is a stored discriminator on a vocabulary D-158 makes explicitly **additive**, whose only reader (S5's own `inst-au-read`, the Auditor-only trail surface) does not exist, in a greenfield store, so a document choosing `edit` over `update` costs a rename and not a contract change.
- **Ratified, and the ratification's condition is this entry.** The product owner ratified the implementation's act — writing six records under three verbs no document declared — on one condition: **the debt lands in S5 §6 in this documentation wave.** Three stored tokens that no document declares would otherwise be found by the next reader, or by the next `spec-check` P3 pass. Not flagged for veto: the vocabulary is additive by D-158's own text and nothing here narrows a stated MUST — it enables one.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): nothing on the records or the verbs, which are written and now declared. One residue of the same audit work is **not** closed by this entry and is D-178's: `correlation_id` is `None` on every authoring record.
- **Extended by D-180 (2026-08-03)**: this entry's closure rule was written against the **authoring** plane and audited against six mutations there. Applied to the **approval** store it leaves two surfaces uncovered — the change unit's opening and its human withdraw — so `submit` and `withdraw` join the roster. The lesson generalises the rule's own wording: *no writer without a token* is audited against the surfaces this design set specifies, and a sweep over one plane is not a sweep.
- **Propagated**: S5 §6 (the `action` bullet + the three-verb paragraph carrying the closure rule); S1 §3.7 (the `pricing_audit_log` bullet); PRD `fr-audit-completeness`; the D-158 entry carries the amendment pointer.

#### D-176 [M] A precondition is evaluated inside the transaction that performs the mutation it guards

- **Where**: [`design/01-foundation.md`](./design/01-foundation.md) §3.3 (the precondition block); [`design/02-plan-definition.md`](./design/02-plan-definition.md) §5; [PRD](./PRD.md) `fr-concurrent-edit`.
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-03 while closing the implementation's debt against this set): this set calls the `If-Match` mechanism a **compare-and-swap** in three places — S2 §5's tag paragraph, S1 §4.2's closed input list (D-155 clause 2, which *relies* on "the draft revision row: its own compare-and-swap" as a held mechanism), and §4.3 — and never says **where** the comparison happens, which is the whole of its strength. The gap has one live instance and it is not hypothetical. A `PATCH` on a plan resolves one of two revisions (D-170); on the arm where the plan is published and holds no draft, the mutation's first write is an **insert** — a successor revision **copied from** the current one — so there is no row for a swap to ride on, and the caller's tag is compared before the inserting transaction opens. A concurrent publish landing in that window makes some other revision current; the successor is then copied from a revision the caller never read, and the call answers as though its precondition had held. That is D-145's lost update arriving through the **copy source** rather than the row version — the same defect D-170 closed one component over, on the arm D-170 did not reach. The implementation states the window in the handler's own doc and reports it, because closing it is a repository signature change.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — one rule and two consequences. **The rule**: a precondition is satisfied only by a comparison made **inside** the transaction that writes the mutation it guards; a comparison made before that transaction opens is a **hint**. For an UPDATE that is the compare-and-swap the set already names, the tag matched in the statement that writes. For an INSERT the tag names a *different* row than the one written, and the rule is the same shape: the guarding row's identity and version are re-read inside the writing transaction and compared there. This gear has exactly one such mutation today, named above. **(1)** The successor open and the facet write that one call performs are **one transaction**: two transactions cannot carry one precondition. The intermediate state is not harmless — an open draft at `max+1` occupies the plan's single editable slot (`uq_pricing_plan_open_draft`, §3.7), and until somebody abandons or publishes it **every** subsequent `PATCH` from **any** operator takes the open-draft arm instead of the successor arm, from a call that answered with an error. **(2)** The audit record being inside its own transaction (D-135) is a **separate** obligation and does not discharge this one: the record says a revision was minted, not that the caller's precondition was true when it was. Rejected: **(a)** leaving the comparison outside and documenting the window — a documented lost update is still a lost update, and the set's own word for the mechanism is a swap; **(b)** fusing the two transactions **without** giving the insert its expected version, which closes the partial outcome and leaves the window exactly where it is, and would relocate three reads and an error rendering into an infra transaction to buy half a fix that reads as a whole one; **(c)** `SERIALIZABLE`, declined on the merits §4.2 already declines it (D-155 clause 2) — it buys nothing the tags buy and holds predicate locks across paths this gear keeps deliberately short.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): the successor arm. `PlanRepo::open_revision` takes the expected version (and the source revision it copies from), checks them inside its own transaction, and the facet write joins that transaction. The implementation deliberately did **not** fuse the two transactions when it audited the open, on the ground that fusing alone leaves the window untouched — which is rejected alternative (b) above and is correct; this entry is what says the two acts are one act rather than a judgement left to whichever group arrives next. The signature change is a **RevisionTarget**-shaped bundle across four repository methods that already carry `#[allow(clippy::too_many_arguments)]`, which the implementation also has filed.
- **Propagated**: S1 §3.3 (the new normative paragraph, beside D-171's precondition mapping); S2 §5 (the successor arm's paragraph, after the tag shape); PRD `fr-concurrent-edit`.

#### D-177 [H] Two Slice-10 primitives are refused on every authoring path, and that refusal is the only thing holding the publish freeze

- **Where**: [`design/10-advanced-primitives.md`](./design/10-advanced-primitives.md) §3 + §6; [`design/01-foundation.md`](./design/01-foundation.md) §4.4 (the `ep-1` log); [`design/12-operator-efficiency.md`](./design/12-operator-efficiency.md) §3 (bulk import).
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-03 while closing the implementation's debt against this set; the most severe item that audit found and the one nobody had recorded): `includedAllowance` (D-45) and `tierQualificationWindow` (D-40) are columns of `pricing_price` declared in S10 §6, and the REST surface that landed at the phase boundary **accepts** both. Ten refusals stand between an authored value and a legal one — `ALLOWANCE_ON_NON_USAGE`, `ALLOWANCE_DOUBLE_FREE`, `ALLOWANCE_ON_NON_SUM`, `ALLOWANCE_QUANTITY_INVALID`, `ALLOWANCE_KIND_UNSUPPORTED`, `ALLOWANCE_WITH_RESERVATION`, `TIER_QUAL_ON_NON_TIERED`, `TIER_QUAL_WINDOW_INCOMPATIBLE`, `TIER_QUAL_ZERO_BAND_LOCK` and `FIXTURE_MISSING` — and **none of the ten existed**, nor did the allowance compile that gives a value its meaning (`inst-ac-band`, `inst-ac-marker`, `inst-ac-carry`) or the rate lock (`inst-tt-lock`). The value was stored, echoed back on the read, projected into the read-model delta and rostered in `ep-1`; the only thing that read either field was D-129's supersession guard, which asserts they must not **change** and never that they were legal. `inst-tt-forbidden` states the reason this is a defect rather than a gap, in as many words: *fail-closed — the field is meaningless there, and an accepted-but-ignored value would mask authoring errors.* One qualification changes the shape of the fix and not its urgency: nothing is frozen **today**, because `POST /bss-pricing/v1/plans/{planId}/publish` is not a mounted route — it is frozen the day Slice 5 mounts it, with no further code change and no gate that would notice.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — refuse at authoring until the rules land, in four clauses. **(1) Refused, not ignored**: a non-null value in either field is a **malformed request** under the Foundation validation envelope (400, **no code minted** — the class D-141 settled), on **every** authoring path — the price create and the price `PATCH` today, and Slice 12's **bulk import** when it lands, which is a second authoring path onto the same draft plane (D-118) and is bound by this rule rather than exempt from it. **(2) The two members stay modelled and the two fields stay rostered**: deleting a member is *worse* than refusing a value, because D-174 clause (1) puts a member this gear does not model outside the idempotency digest and therefore **replays** it, converting a refused field into a silently ignored one; and removing either field from `ep-1` would bump a generation for a field nobody can author, which is the fabrication D-162 refuses. **(3) The refusal is load-bearing, and its removal has an order**: it may be deleted only in the change that lands the ten rules **and** the compile — not before, and not by a group tidying what looks like an unreachable branch. The group that mounts the publish route inherits this as a precondition to check rather than a detail: at that moment publish becomes the freezing act, and it is safe only while every authoring path still refuses. **(4) One of the ten cannot be built even then**: `FIXTURE_MISSING` on the `trailing_tier` variant needs that variant registered in the **joint** conformance registry, which is a counterparty act with Rating (D-60) and not pricing's to mint alone. Rejected: **building the ten gates now, without the compile.** A `graduated` row with `includedAllowance {100, none}` passes all six `inst-ac-gate` rules and publishes; no `$0 [0, N)` band is prepended, no authored band offset, no marker materialized, no `pricing_plan_grant` row exists for a `carry` policy — so Rating bills the first hundred units at the authored rate and the operator is charged for an allowance that was accepted, **validated**, and silently ignored. A gate without its compile manufactures exactly the false assurance the set refuses elsewhere for unlanded facts (D-149 clause 3, D-161 clause 1, D-167 clause 3, D-168 clause 1). And unlike the gate, this posture is **reversible at zero cost**: when Slice 10 lands the refusal is deleted and the rules replace it.
- **The PRD's requirements are untouched.** This decides which surface refuses what *while the slice is unbuilt*; it weakens no `MUST` and defers no capability, so it is **not flagged for veto**. D-40, D-45, D-59, D-60 and D-130 all stand exactly as decided.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): nothing on the REST refusal, which is built at the request→domain conversion with five tests and a guard-by-removal proof, and whose reader-facing warning now sits in all three files whose reader could cause the freeze. Two things are owed elsewhere: the **bulk-import** arm, owed by Slice 12; and the `trailing_tier` **joint fixture** — `bss_fixtures::Variant` has four members and none is `trailing_tier`, and the gate's `required_variants` never asks for one, so `inst-tt-fixture`'s publish block has neither code nor variant to name. That is a **new** entry on the standing list of owed cross-gear work, jointly with **Rating**, whose own SEAMS M12 already carries it as open ("plus the joint fixture") while asserting that pricing carries the variant.
- **Propagated**: S10 §3 (the four-clause normative paragraph after `inst-tt-joint`) + §6 (the Slice-10 column table's preamble — declared, not yet authorable); S1 §4.4 (why the `ep-1` roster keeps two fields nothing can author); S12 §3 (the bulk-import Error Scenario).

#### D-178 [M] The correlation id has one producer for the whole gear, and no document named it

- **Where**: [`design/05-governance.md`](./design/05-governance.md) §3 (`inst-au-complete`) + §6 (the writer contract); [`design/01-foundation.md`](./design/01-foundation.md) §3.7 (`pricing_outbox`); [PRD](./PRD.md) `fr-audit-completeness`.
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-03 while closing the implementation's debt against this set): `inst-au-complete` lists the **correlation id** among the five fields an audit record must carry, at `p1`, and §3.7's `pricing_outbox` bullet requires a correlation key on every event. **No document in this set names a producer for either.** The string appears exactly twice in the whole design set, both times as a field in a list of fields. So the one join both an auditor and an operator need — *this event, that record, one operator call* — rested on nothing, and it is not a cosmetic join: D-135 segments the audit chain per **aggregate**, so a single operator call that edits a plan facet and a price row writes records on two different chains, and the correlation is the only thing that says they were one act. The implementation writes `None` on every authoring record and says so at the point a reader meets it; the publish path has a value only because its caller is in-process and passes one.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — the correlation id is the **request's**, and it has one producer for the gear. **(1)** It is the request-scoped correlation the gear's HTTP edge establishes: the value the platform propagates inbound when there is one, **minted at the edge when there is not** — so the field is always satisfiable, is never NULL on `pricing_outbox`, and is never absent from a record written on a request path. **(2)** Every audit record and every outbox row a single operator call produces carries **one** value. An in-process producer supplies its own, which is what the publish commit's parameter already is. **(3)** It is **not** the `Idempotency-Key` — client-minted, per-operation, and the subject of a different comparison (§3.7) — and it is not derived from the payload; conflating them would make a retried call correlate to the original and an unretried sibling correlate to nothing. **What is deliberately left to the platform** is the **spelling** of the propagated value's transport: every gear's trail wants the same header, so naming one here would be this gear legislating a platform contract. What is *not* left open is the requirement or the fallback, which is what an implementer was missing. Rejected: **(a)** requiring the caller to supply it in a header, which would put a third required header on every mutation (D-171 having just enumerated two) for a field whose only reader is the Auditor, and would refuse a **correct** mutation for omitting it — the shape D-174 rejected for the digest; **(b)** making the field optional, which narrows a `p1` MUST, deletes the cross-chain join the field exists for, and carries product flavour this decision has no mandate for.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): the plumbing. Nothing establishes a request correlation in an Axum handler in this gear, so `audit_stamp` writes `None` and says it is a gap rather than a decision. This is the residue that keeps the audit work "substantially closed" rather than closed, and it is one value threaded from the edge to the two writers that already take a stamp. **Paid 2026-08-03** — an edge layer establishes the value once before any handler runs and it reaches the writers through the `AuditStamp`; see D-181's owed-back clause.
- **Amended by D-181 (2026-08-03)**: this entry left the transport's **spelling** to the platform on the reading that this gear had nothing to consume. It has: the platform's canonical-error middleware already extracts a W3C trace id, this gear already mounts it, and the primary spelling fits the column. D-181 records that the value is nonetheless **minted, not adopted** — a trace names a trace and not an operator call — and names the platform-side change (`toolkit` exposing the extracted id as a request extension) that would make the question a real choice. Clauses (1)–(3) of this entry are unchanged.
- **Propagated**: S5 §3 (`inst-au-complete`) + §6 (the writer contract's field list); S1 §3.7 (the `pricing_outbox` bullet); PRD `fr-audit-completeness`.

#### D-179 [H] The freezing act needs a code, and the authoring refusal deliberately has none

- **Where**: [`design/01-foundation.md`](./design/01-foundation.md) §3.3 (the code, beside its siblings) + §4.2 (the rule in the Foundation publish set); [`design/10-advanced-primitives.md`](./design/10-advanced-primitives.md) §3 (beside D-177's four clauses); [`design/12-operator-efficiency.md`](./design/12-operator-efficiency.md) §3 (the bulk-import arm inherits it).
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-03 while planning the phase that mounts the publish route — D-177 named the hazard and left the surface that stops it unnamed): D-177 clause (1) refuses a non-null `includedAllowance` or `tierQualificationWindow` **at authoring**, as a malformed request under the validation envelope with **no code minted** — the class D-141 settled. Clause (3) then hands the group that mounts `POST …/plans/{planId}/publish` a precondition to check, because "it is frozen the day Slice 5 mounts it, with no further code change and no gate that would notice". But **a stored row reaching publish is not the act clause (1) refuses.** The request is well-formed, its preconditions hold, the value is already in the store, and the operator's remedy is a different one — clear the stored field, not reshape the payload. Three routes put a value there that no authoring refusal covers: rows authored before the refusal landed; the **bulk-import** arm D-177 itself records as owed by Slice 12 and therefore absent; and any write that did not come through a handler. A gate that is fail-closed for all three cannot depend on knowing which. And the refusal is **not expressible without a code on this path at all**: an authoring refusal can be codeless because the envelope has a no-code arm (`InvalidRequest` → `invalid_argument` with a constraint), while a publish refusal travels as a `Violation` in the validation report, which carries a code by construction — an empty report short-circuits to an internal fault, so a codeless publish refusal arrives as a 500 and tells the operator nothing.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — mint **`PRIMITIVE_RULES_UNBUILT`** (422 by the set's convention; the canonical family has no 422 category so it renders 400 with the code as the discriminator), registered into the **Foundation** publish rule set beside `ROUNDING_POLICY_UNRESOLVED`, and blocking. Four clauses. **(1) It names the reason, not the field.** Two fields carry it today and a third rostered-but-unauthorable field would carry the same one; a per-field code would multiply with the roster and every member would be deleted again when Slice 10 lands. The operator's action is identical whichever field it is — D-146's line. **(2) It tells the operator the truth**: the value is not wrong, the machinery that would judge it does not exist yet. `inst-tt-forbidden`'s stated reason is that an accepted-but-ignored value masks authoring errors; a code saying *unbuilt* is the honest rendering of that, where a generic invalidity code would assert a judgement no rule made. **(3) Its removal has D-177(3)'s order**, unchanged: it is deleted in the same change that lands the ten S10 refusals **and** the allowance compile, not before, and not by a group tidying an unreachable branch. **(4) It is stable across re-entry**: a re-submitted publish reads the same stored row and takes the same refusal — the gate consumes nothing, clears nothing, and does not depend on being the first to run. Rejected: **(a)** no code, matching the authoring posture — not expressible on the publish path, as above, and it converts a refusal the operator can act on into a 500; **(b)** `LIFECYCLE_FORBIDDEN` — D-146 narrowed that code to refusals describing no alternative action, and this one describes a specific one; **(c)** leaving publish ungated and resting on the authoring refusal, which is the D-177 hazard restated: that posture holds only while **every** authoring path refuses, and D-177 records one that does not exist yet.
- **The PRD's requirements are untouched.** This names the surface and the code for a refusal D-177 already required; it weakens no `MUST` and defers no capability, so it is **not flagged for veto**. D-40, D-45, D-141, D-146 and D-177 all stand exactly as decided.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): nothing beyond the group that carries this wave — the rule, its registration and a guard-by-removal proof. The **bulk-import** arm stays owed by Slice 12 exactly as D-177 recorded it, and inherits this code rather than minting a second one.
- **Propagated**: S1 §3.3 (the code declaration) + §4.2 (the Foundation publish rule set); S10 §3 (the clause after D-177's four); S12 §3 (the bulk-import Error Scenario).

#### D-180 [M] The approval plane owes two `action` tokens — `submit` and `withdraw` (D-175 closure rule, applied to the store it was not written against)

- **Where**: [`design/05-governance.md`](./design/05-governance.md) §6 (the `action` roster + the approval-plane paragraph after D-175's); [`design/01-foundation.md`](./design/01-foundation.md) §3.7 (`pricing_audit_log`); [PRD](./PRD.md) `fr-audit-completeness`.
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-03 while building this slice's approval surfaces, and reported rather than absorbed): D-175 wrote the closure rule — **no writer without a token** — against the *authoring* plane, and it was audited against the six authoring mutations. Applied to the **approval** store, which D-175 did not sweep, it leaves two surfaces uncovered. §4's initial state opens a unit with the *"submitter recorded"* and §8's `dod-audit` obliges *"every mutation MUST record actor/timestamp/before-after/approval trail"*, and an insert into `pricing_approval` is a mutation; `inst-as-void` specifies `POST …/approvals/{approvalId}/withdraw`, on its own mounted route, and calls it **audited** in as many words. The roster named neither. The implementation wrote both records and **minted the two verbs itself**, reporting them as minted in four places rather than treating them as declared, for exactly D-175's reason: D-158's instruction is that a slice adding an audited record adds its token *here*, and a code group may not edit this document. This is D-175's situation reproduced, and D-175 was ratified on the condition that its debt land in the next documentation wave — this one.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — four clauses. **(1) Both verbs are declared**, with their writers named: `submit` is written by the **non-committing arm** of `POST /bss-pricing/v1/plans/{planId}/publish` — the arm that evaluates materiality, inserts the pinned unit and answers `202` — and `withdraw` by `POST /bss-pricing/v1/approvals/{approvalId}/withdraw`. **(2) The two warrants are not equally strong, and the weaker one is stated at its real strength.** `withdraw`'s is strong: `inst-as-void` names the surface and calls it audited. `submit`'s is **`dod-audit` and nothing wider** — the earlier citation of `inst-tp-record` is **withdrawn**, because §6 assigns that instruction to `approve`/`reject` and its requirement is met by the submitter principal standing on those two records whether or not a `submit` token exists; nor is the submit a surface the endpoint map overlooked, the route being already `plan × publish` and already named by `publish`. What the roster lacked was a verb for the *other* thing that route does. Re-inflating the warrant would hand the roster a claim the debt does not carry. **(3) The machine-driven void stays unrecorded, and that boundary is normative rather than an omission**: `inst-ap-pin`'s TOCTOU guard closes a unit from inside the transaction of the mutation that invalidated it, and that mutation has already written its record on the **same** segment (D-135 keys both on the plan), at the same instant, under the actor who caused it. A second record would need an actor the act does not have, and `inst-au-pii` makes the actor a fact. So `withdraw` means *a principal withdrew this* and an absent record against a `voided` unit means *the guard closed it* — a distinction an auditor can read and a synthetic principal would destroy. **(4) Both closure rules are re-audited in both directions and hold**: with these two, every mutating and attempted-mutating surface this set specifies carries a verb; and `retire`, `backdate_import` and `policy_update` stay declared because `inst-rt-cancel`, `inst-bd-store` and D-10 *specify* those writers — the companion rule bars a token no **specified** surface writes, never one whose code is merely unbuilt (the D-179-wave clarification in §6). Rejected: **(a)** folding the withdraw into `reject`, a different edge by a different authority with a mandatory reason, or into `abandon`, D-145's plan-draft flip on the other plane — either would make two acts indistinguishable in the one store D-12 confines to the Auditor; **(b)** leaving the submit unrecorded on the ground that the route already has `publish`, which would put the change unit's *opening* — the act the two-person rule turns on — outside the trail while `dod-audit` obliges it; **(c)** recording the guard's void under `withdraw` too, which buys one token's economy by asserting a principal that did not act.
- **What the second run reads, and what no consumer reads.** `inst-as-immutable` makes a decided or voided record immutable and a re-submit open a **new** one, so the second run of this mechanism writes a second record rather than mutating the first: a withdraw followed by a fresh submit leaves `submit`, `withdraw`, `submit` in sequence on one plan's segment, which is the history an auditor needs and not a duplicate to collapse. Neither verb is consumer-visible — an `action` is a stored discriminator, no publish unit carries it, and its only reading surface is `inst-au-read`'s Auditor-only trail, which this gear has not built. The sibling surface is S1 §3.7's `pricing_audit_log` bullet, where the roster is summarized; both verbs are named there.
- **Not flagged for veto.** The vocabulary is additive by D-158's own text; nothing here narrows a stated MUST — it enables one — and the only reader of these tokens does not exist yet, in a greenfield store, so a document preferring `void` over `withdraw` would have cost a rename and not a contract change. Ratified on the same footing as D-175, whose condition this entry discharges.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): nothing. Both records are written inside their own mutations' transactions, both verbs are now declared, and the module that minted them can drop its "owed to S5 §6" section.
- **Propagated**: S5 §6 (the `action` roster + the approval-plane paragraph); S1 §3.7 (the `pricing_audit_log` bullet); PRD `fr-audit-completeness`.

#### D-181 [M] The correlation id is a UUID this gear mints; the platform's inbound trace id fits the column and is still not adopted (D-178 amendment)

- **Where**: [`design/05-governance.md`](./design/05-governance.md) §3 (`inst-au-complete`) + §6 (the writer contract's field list); [`design/01-foundation.md`](./design/01-foundation.md) §3.7 (`pricing_outbox`); [PRD](./PRD.md) `fr-audit-completeness`.
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-03 while building the correlation edge D-178 owed): D-178 named the producer and deliberately left the **spelling** of the propagated value's transport to the platform, on the ground that naming one would be this gear legislating a platform contract. Building the edge turned that deferral into two questions the entry does not answer, and the implementation reported both rather than answering them by writing values. **(1) May a non-UUID 128-bit value occupy `correlation_id`?** The column is `uuid` on Postgres in both stores, and the platform *does* have an inbound convention: `toolkit`'s canonical-error middleware reads W3C `traceparent` → `x-trace-id` → `x-request-id`, and this gear already mounts it around its whole merged router. A W3C trace id is 32 hex characters — 128 bits, exactly the column's width — and parses through `Uuid::parse_str` and renders back byte-identically. So "the shape does not fit" was **false** of the primary spelling and true only of the two free-text fallbacks. **But it is not a UUID**: probed in-crate, `ffffffff…` lands as version `Max` / variant `Future`, while this column is minted **v7** and read as time-ordered. **(2) Trace-vs-call granularity.** A trace id names a whole distributed trace, which a batch caller may hold across hundreds of calls; D-178 clause (2) binds the value to **one operator call**.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-03** — three clauses. **(1) The value is a v7 UUID this gear mints**, and a non-UUID 128-bit value does **not** occupy the column: two kinds of value in one column with nothing distinguishing them defeats the time-ordering the Auditor read surface is meant to index on, and the design set has nowhere to record which kind a given row holds. **(2) An inbound trace id is not adopted**, and the reason is granularity rather than shape. Adopting it would make D-178 clause (2) — *"every record and every outbox row of a single operator call carries one value"* — a property of the **caller's** instrumentation rather than of this gear, with no header saying which the caller meant; and because only one of the three spellings fits the column, adoption would give a trail↔trace join that holds for callers who send `traceparent` and silently does not for callers who send `x-request-id`, who would see one value on an error response and a different, minted one on the trail with nothing saying they disagree. A join that is right sometimes is worse than one that is uniformly this gear's. The consequence reaches past the trail: the correlation key rides the `pricing_outbox` rows this gear emits to Tariffs, Rating, Subscriptions and Billing, so adopting a caller's trace id would put the caller's identifier on a consumer-visible field. **(3) The platform-side fix is named rather than built here**: what would close the propagation half cleanly is `toolkit` exposing its **already-extracted** trace id as a **request extension**, so one place decides the three-spelling precedence and no gear grows a third copy of `parse_w3c_trace_id` (there are two today — `toolkit`'s and `toolkit-http`'s, the first a deliberate duplicate of the second). With that extension in place the question reopens as a real choice; until then D-178 clause (1)'s "the value the platform propagates inbound when there is one" has no gear-side reading that does not amount to this gear inventing the convention. Rejected: **(a)** adopting `traceparent` only, which is (2)'s partial join with the partiality left undocumented; **(b)** widening the column to text to admit all three spellings, which is the free-text option D-178 clause (a) already rejected and which would drop the time-ordering; **(c)** carrying both — a minted correlation *and* an adopted trace id in a second column — which is a real option and a **different** decision, needing a column on two ≥ 7-year append-only stores and a reader that does not exist, for a join nothing in this set has yet asked for.
- **Not flagged for veto.** Both clauses narrow nothing: D-178's requirement, its fallback and its clause (3) stand exactly as decided, and what changes is that a deferral now says *what was checked and why the reachable option was declined* instead of implying nothing was reachable. The one statement this corrects is D-178's own implicit premise, not a MUST.
- **What the second run reads.** A retry under the same `Idempotency-Key` is answered from the dedup store and executes nothing, so it writes no record and adds no correlation — the trail keeps the original call's value, which is the intended reading. The one path that executes twice under one key is D-142 clause (4)'s **takeover of an expired claim**, and its records carry the takeover request's own correlation: two executions, two correlations, one idempotency key — which is D-178 clause (3) working, not a gap.
- **Owed back to the implementation** (`bss/pricing-impl`): nothing in this gear. D-178's owed half is **paid** — the correlation is established once by an edge layer before any handler runs, reaches the writers through the `AuditStamp`, and a route mounted without that layer answers an internal fault rather than minting a per-record value, so the failure mode is a loud wiring defect instead of a silently broken join. What remains is the **platform** item in clause (3), which is `toolkit`'s and not this gear's, and it is a new entry on the standing list of owed cross-boundary work rather than an unpaid clause here.
- **Propagated**: S5 §3 (`inst-au-complete`) + §6 (the writer contract's field list); S1 §3.7 (the `pricing_outbox` bullet); PRD `fr-audit-completeness`.

#### D-182 [M] The trailing-void exemption's decider is a lane that was never built, and D-131 already decides what a silent lane means

- **Where**: [`design/07-pricewindow-linkage.md`](./design/07-pricewindow-linkage.md) §3 (`inst-fg-trailing`, the trailing-void step of future-gap detection); [`design/11-lifecycle.md`](./design/11-lifecycle.md) §2 (`inst-rt-cancel`, which consumes the same lane for the same per-key predicate).
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-04 while building the three window routes — the surfaces `inst-fg-trailing` names as its callers, and the first that can reach the rule at all): `inst-fg-trailing` requires every cancel and every `effectiveTo` shortening to leave the key covered through `max(current coverage end, now + the longest billing cycle sold on that key)`, and its **only** exemption — D-51's, narrowed by D-80 and by D-94's conjunction — turns on whether the key has in-flight subscribers, a predicate that resolves through the **D-79 Subscriptions inbound lane**. In this repository that lane does not exist in any form: `gears/bss/subscriptions` holds a `docs` directory and **no crate at all**, this gear's client hub wires an authz resolver and a types registry and nothing else, and no presence read, contract type or route exists anywhere in the tree. That much was known when the phase was planned. What the phase found, and nobody had recorded, is that **the counterparty's own design set specifies the shape D-131 rejected**: SUB-P8 is authored on their side (`design/09-consumer-contracts.md` §4.1, their PRD §9.1) as the **count** of non-terminal subscriptions over the submitted price-id set — one aggregate over the union — while D-131 makes the response a **per-price-id presence map** precisely because a single count answers only "does this plan have any subscriber at all", under which retirement would cancel nothing whenever one key is occupied. No Subscriptions document cites D-131, and none names a path, a request or response schema, or the timeout whose expiry D-131 makes the fail-closed case. So the exemption is not waiting on plumbing over an agreed contract; the contract that would decide it is still the one D-131 replaced. That leaves an implementer mounting a rule one arm of which has no operand, and the reading is not forced: D-131 states the safe direction for a lane that **exists and stops answering**, and read literally "outage or timeout" is not satisfied by a lane that was never built — an implementer could as easily read an absent lane as an absent *predicate* and treat the exemption as vacuously true, which is the fail-open reading and is the one that sells into the void D-62 → D-80 → D-94 spent three waves closing.
- **Decision**: **2026-08-04** — the refusal stands unexempted, and it is a rule rather than a stub. **Five clauses. (1) An absent lane is D-131's fail-closed case.** What fail-closed is about is the predicate's **evaluability**, not the mechanism by which it became unevaluable: a lane with no client is no more informative than one that timed out, and distinguishing "never built" from "not answering" would make the safe direction depend on the reason for the silence rather than on the silence. So every cancel and every `effectiveTo` shortening that would leave the key uncovered through `max(current coverage end, now + the longest billing cycle sold on that key)` is refused — **including the ones the design set exempts** — with **`WINDOW_TRAILING_VOID`**, naming the key and the first uncovered instant, exactly as `inst-fg-trailing` already specifies, and evaluated inside the mutating transaction as `inst-fg-when` requires. **(2) No code is minted, and no exemption branch is written.** `WINDOW_TRAILING_VOID` already carries this refusal in S7 §5, and a second code saying *the lane is missing* would multiply the roster with the **reason** where the operator's remedy is identical either way — D-146's line, applied as D-179 clause (1) applies it. Nor does the implementation carry a disabled exemption arm: a branch nothing can reach is a branch the next group deletes as dead code without learning what it was for, so the exemption's shape is recorded **here**, where the group landing the lane will read it, and not as an unreachable `if`. **(3) The sibling surface is Slice 11's retirement, and it inherits this refusal with the opposite sign.** `inst-rt-cancel` consumes the same lane for the same per-key predicate at the opposite polarity — it cancels not-yet-active windows only on keys with **no** in-flight subscribers, and fail-closed there means the windows are **kept**. With no lane, retirement therefore cancels **nothing**: every key reads as occupied, where the design set would have let it cancel on the empty ones. One absent lane, two opposite-signed consequences, both safe in direction (coverage is preserved on either surface) and neither what the documents describe. It is named here rather than left for the slice that implements retirement to meet as a surprise. **(4) The refusal carries no publish unit and nothing consumer-visible reads it.** It fires inside the mutating transaction and **before** the pending `CatalogVersion` ref D-99 requires, so no version is requested, no plan subject is re-projected and no reading surface — the sellability surface included — can observe the rule at all: what a consumer sees is the coverage that was never removed. A refusal with **no** reader is a different animal from one whose reader is merely unnamed, and this one has none by construction. **(5) It is stable across re-entry.** A re-submitted cancel reads the same stored coverage and takes the same refusal: the check consumes nothing, clears nothing and does not depend on being the first to run, so the second run of the mechanism is identical to the first rather than a different answer over a spent input. **Its removal is gated on the counterparty twice over**, and neither gate is this gear's to meet: the refusal is deleted by the change that lands the D-79 lane client — at which point the exemption becomes reachable **for the first time** and needs its own tests, nothing ever having exercised it — and that change presupposes both that Subscriptions builds the read (they have no crate to hold it) and that SUB-P8 first moves to D-131's per-price-id map, the union count their set specifies being unable to answer the per-key question every consumer of the lane asks. Both halves are already on the standing list of owed cross-gear adoptions; what is new here is the record that this gear **refuses in the meantime** rather than waiting.
