Committing to exhaustiveness instead stays available, but needs the mechanical propagation check D-68's standing consequence asks for.
- **[M] The projector's pending-scan fairness policy** (added 2026-08-03 from the read-side build, the residue D-163 clause (2) does not close). D-163 bounds the scan **per tenant** so that one tenant's stuck backlog cannot defer another's completions, and that is the correctness half. What it does not settle is **how many tenants a pass takes and in what order** — a bound is needed either way (an unbounded backlog read into memory turns a transient registry outage into a memory problem), and any bound starves somebody under sustained load. The candidates are a round-robin over tenants with pending refs, or a per-tenant cap with a deployment-wide pass budget; both need a **value**, and no document in this set states either. The item is more pressing than a throughput note because D-163 clause (2) makes a saturated scan defer completions as well as warms: under sustained overload the frontier lags, loudly (`pin_eligibility_overdue`) and safely, but it lags. A fix belongs beside the §14 NFR set, where the gear's other bounds are ratified. **Narrowed 2026-08-03 by what the implementation built** (which settles the shape and not the value): the projector took the **second** candidate — a per-tenant cap with a deployment-wide pass budget — and the round-robin is not built. So what remains open is (i) the **budget's value**, provisionally 250 tenants per pass with its reasoning stated in the gear's config and explicitly the owner's to ratify, and (ii) the **order**, which is ascending tenant id and is **not rotating** — so past the bound the tail of the tenant order is not swept *at all* on any pass, rather than swept late. That is sharper than the "any bound starves somebody" wording above and it is the half that needs the owner: making it rotate needs a cursor no table in §3.7 carries, so it is a schema addition and not a tuning knob.
- **[M] No default role can withdraw an approval unit** (added 2026-08-03 from the approval-surface build, and **verified against the documents** while landing D-180 — recorded rather than decided, because widening a gate is the owner's call). S5 §6's transition `inst-as-void` names the withdrawer as *"the submitter (or a `CatalogAdmin`)"*. The AuthZ catalog's endpoint map has **one** row for the whole path — `GET/POST /bss-pricing/v1/approvals*` → `approval × read` / `approve` — so a `POST` here is `approve`; and the role matrix in the same section gives `CatalogAdmin` `approval × read` and **deliberately not** `approval × approve` (*"it publishes, it does not approve itself"*), while a submitter holds `plan × publish` under `FinanceManager` or `CatalogAdmin`, neither of which carries `approve` either. So under the default matrix the only principal who can reach the withdraw is a **`FinanceReviewer`** — precisely neither of the two the transition names, and the role the two-person rule is built to keep on the other side of the submission. The implementation gates on `approve` as the map says and reports the contradiction rather than widening the gate; gating on `read` instead would let anyone who can *see* a unit close it, which is worse and is not what the map says. Three candidate closures, none free: give the path its own `approval × withdraw` action (a catalog addition, and the shape the resource table's *"each action sits on its real object"* rule prefers); grant `CatalogAdmin` `approval × approve` (which the matrix's own SoD note refuses in as many words); or narrow `inst-as-void` to the reviewer, which changes who may unstick a pinned scope key and is a product call. Nothing is broken today — the route is mounted and enforces what the map says — but a tenant provisioning only the default roles cannot execute a transition this design set specifies.
- **Not verified, carried from the review**: `billingTiming` has no counterparty to disagree with until the Billing gear has a doc set (D-48's Billing countersign, still the only thing keeping G-4 open).

## H. Tooling / traceability notes

- **cfs traceability exclusion for the design-slice sets (2026-07-28 review, finding 15).** The BSS multi-slice design sets (`gears/bss/*/docs/design/*.md` — pricing 12, rating 16, subscriptions 9 slices) are **deliberately outside cfs autodetect/traceability**: cfs hardcodes its artifact-kind set, so a `DESIGN_SLICE` kind cannot be registered via the kit manifest (attempted + reverted 2026-07-28), and a brace-glob on the DESIGN pattern breaks `DESIGN.md` matching. The slice docs follow the DESIGN template, declare `cpt-cf-bss-*` ids, and are review-audited (2026-07-22/28 waves) instead of cfs-validated. The exclusion itself is the `[[ignore]]` block in `.cf-studio/config/artifacts.toml`, which points here. **Revisit when cfs supports custom artifact kinds.**

#### D-247 [H] The set never said **which** subject an approval unit re-derives, and the silence produced one defect three times

- **Where**: [`design/05-governance.md`](./design/05-governance.md) `inst-ap-pin`; `pricing/src/infra/approval.rs` (`re_derive`).
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-07 by the Slice 11 strand, while extending a census that *executes* rather than asserts): `inst-ap-pin` says a unit pins its subject's content and re-derives it at decision time. It never said which subject — the draft under review, or the plan as it currently stands. `re_derive` picks the re-assembly by `subject_kind`, and a retirement unit carries `plan_revision`, whose arm assembles the plan's **open draft**. A retirement's subject is the **published** revision, so a plan with no open draft assembled to `NotFound`, `content_matches_pin` answered `false`, and **every retirement unit was openable and never approvable** — `APPROVAL_CONTENT_MISMATCH` on the first attempt to decide it.
- **It is the third instance of one defect in one function, and the previous two are written into it.** The `window` arm records that assembling a published subject as a draft "would answer `NotFound` for every window unit in existence"; the supersession arm records finding it again on 2026-08-06, with the reason it went unseen — no test decided a supersession unit until D-88's orchestrator had one to decide. Same route, third arm. **Each instance was invisible until something first tried to *decide* a unit of that kind**, which is why three separate builds each shipped it.
- **Decision (2026-08-07): state the rule normatively.** A unit whose subject is a **published fact** — window, supersession, retirement — re-derives the **current revision**; only a plan-revision *authoring* unit re-derives the **open draft**. This is the sentence that would have made all three instances unwritable.
- **The implementation resolves it structurally rather than per kind**, which is the half a document cannot enforce: the current-revision assembly is now one `current_revision_shape` instead of a third hand-maintained copy, and retirement is discriminated by its subject ref rather than by a new `subject_kind`, because the kind was never the thing that was wrong. **Any future unit over a published fact must reuse that assembly or be added to it — a fourth copy is how this recurs.**
- **Propagated**: S5 `inst-ap-pin`.

#### D-248 [M] An overlay publish moves two projected subjects and announces neither

- **Where**: `pricing/src/infra/overlay_publish.rs` ("What this deliberately does not do"); [`design/09-price-overlays.md`](./design/09-price-overlays.md) `inst-pl-commit`; [`design/01-foundation.md`](./design/01-foundation.md) `fr-event-contract`'s frozen name set; D-234's residue (2), D-112, D-218.
- **Problem**: since D-234 an overlay publish is its own unit, and since D-112 it re-projects **two** subjects — the overlay itself and the tenant-scoped `overlay_index` shard. Every other projected subject that moves announces on the wire: a plan publish `PlanPublished`, a bundle `BundleUpdated`, a price `PriceCreated`/`PriceUpdated`, a window one of four names. An overlay publish announces **nothing**, so a consumer polling the outbox learns that plans and prices and bundles moved and never learns that the overlay layer applied on top of them did. `overlay_publish.rs` recorded the absence as deliberate-and-deferred rather than forgotten, on D-218's posture that a wire event is the design set's to declare — and D-218 is now decided, so the deferral has run out.
- **Options**: (a) mint one name, `PriceOverlayPublished`, aggregated on the overlay id; (b) reuse an existing name; (c) leave it, on the argument that a consumer re-reads on `CatalogVersionPublished` and per-subject events are redundant.
- **Decision (2026-08-07): (a).** (b) has no candidate — every existing name's subject is a plan, a price, a window or a bundle, and an overlay is none of them. (c) proves too much: it is an argument against `BundleUpdated` and `PlanPublished` equally, and the set has both, so the design's posture is per-subject announcement and this is the one subject missing from it.
- **The aggregate is the overlay id, not a plan**, and that is the one place this differs from `BundleUpdated`. A bundle rides a plan revision, so its event orders within the plan's stream. An overlay is tenant-scoped and **may target no plan at all** (`target_plan_ids: []` is legal and is the global-scope case), so a plan aggregate does not exist to order it within. Ordering per `(tenantId, aggregateId)` therefore means per overlay, which is also the right granularity: two revisions of one overlay must not be observed out of order, and two different overlays have no ordering relationship to preserve.
- **Payload**: `priceOverlayId`, `revision`, `scopeClass`, `scopeValue`, `precedence`, `pendingVersionRef`, `correlationId`. The scope pair is the **`overlay_index` shard key** (D-112), so it tells a consumer which index document to re-read without a lookup; `precedence` is the ordering it resolves by; the version ref is **pending** for `PlanPublishedPayload`'s reason — the version does not exist yet and will not until the registry batches (D-47). Dedup key `PriceOverlayPublished:<overlay_id>:<revision>`, mirroring `BundleUpdated`'s, because a revision publishes once.
- **`fr-event-contract`'s set is described as frozen, and this extends it deliberately** — the fourteenth name, added by decision rather than by a module minting surface for itself, which is exactly the discipline `overlay_publish.rs` was observing when it declined to.
- **The set is frozen in the *schema*, which this entry did not know when it was written, and that is what defers the build.** `m20260802_000009` guards `pricing_outbox` with `CONSTRAINT chk_pricing_outbox_event_name CHECK (event_name IN (…))`, enumerating the thirteen names on **both** engines, and `postgres_schema_stores::every_frozen_event_name_is_enqueueable` drives each one into the table — so the enum and the constraint are pinned to each other by a test that touches the database. A fourteenth name is therefore **a migration**, and on `SQLite` a `CHECK` rewrite is a table rebuild. Found the way the design says to find things: the first run of the new case failed with a **driver** refusal (`code: 275 CHECK constraint failed`) rather than an assertion, which is the signal that a guard exists that nobody had written down here.
- **Built 2026-08-07 on `m20260802_000060`**, a number the product owner released after the deferral above was reported. It sits **above** both strand ranges (Slice 11-migration `000043`–`000049`, Slice 6 `000050`–`000059`), so a collision is impossible by construction rather than by agreement; what the strands owe is a roster line at merge, which they already expect for their own tables.
- **The migration restates the whole list rather than appending one name**, because Postgres can drop and re-add a named constraint and `SQLite` cannot alter a `CHECK` at all. The `SQLite` arm is therefore a table rebuild on `m20260802_000042`'s four-statement shape — and the rebuild recreates **all three indexes**, since `DROP TABLE` takes its indexes with it. Leaving them out would drop `uq_pricing_outbox_dedup_key` silently, and at-least-once delivery with no dedup index is a duplicate-event bug the fast tier would never see.
- **`down` restores thirteen and that is deliberately not symmetric**: rolling back with a `PriceOverlayPublished` row already enqueued fails the restored `CHECK` on Postgres and the rebuild's `INSERT … SELECT` on `SQLite`. The row is a promise made to a consumer; a migration that quietly dropped it would break at-least-once delivery. A rollback past this point is a decision about already-published events, not a schema step.
- **Propagated**: (2026-08-07, as its own change) S1 `fr-event-contract`'s name set (with the note that the set is frozen in **two** places that must agree — the FR row and `chk_pricing_outbox_event_name` — so a name joins both or neither), and S9 `inst-pl-commit`'s event list (the payload, the aggregate and the dedup key). Claimed only now, and the delay is the point: P1 caught this entry claiming propagation on its first draft, before either document had been touched, and a claim that outruns the edit is the defect P1 exists to find.

#### D-249 [H] The overlay stack applies **ascending** — lowest `precedence` first, most specific last

- **Where**: [`design/09-price-overlays.md`](./design/09-price-overlays.md) §3 step 2 (`inst-plv-adjustment`), `inst-plv-class-tiebreak`, §F.1's "Overlay stack sort direction" fork; D-138; D-220; D-230.
- **Problem**: the set states the total order `precedence → class order → overlay id` and never says **ascending or descending**. Before D-138 that was a question about arithmetic order, and `markup`/`discount` commute. D-138 made `fixed` **replace** the running amount, so it discards every layer beneath it — and the direction therefore decides **which layers survive**, not merely the order they are applied in. Two conforming implementations of one published stack differ by the whole of the discarded set. Worked example: layers `10 markup +20%`, `20 discount −5%`, `30 fixed 100` resolve to **100** ascending (the `fixed` is last and erases both) and to **114** descending (the `fixed` is first and erases nothing).
- **Decision (owner, 2026-08-07): ascending.** The stack is applied from the **lowest** `precedence` upward, so the last layer applied is the most specific one and a `fixed` at a high precedence is the operator's way of saying "this price, regardless of what is underneath".
- **Why this reading rather than the other**: `precedence` then means *how late I speak*, which is the only reading under which the class order already written into the set — `customerGroup > partner > orgTier > brand > region > global`, declared least-specific-first — composes with it rather than fighting it. Under the descending reading the most specific layer would be applied first and be the one most likely erased, which inverts what "most specific wins" means everywhere else in this gear.
- **This is a joint contract and the countersign is owed.** Evaluation is **Tariffs-owned**; the catalog publishes the stack and does not apply it. This entry settles pricing's side and unblocks the two decisions that were waiting on it, on the D-48 pattern where a decision stands while a counterparty's countersign is still outstanding. **Owed: Tariffs' adoption**, and the §F.1 row stays until it lands.
- **What it unblocks**: [D-220](#d-220-h-d-138s-authoring-warning-has-no-computable-condition-until-f1-decides-the-stacks-sort-direction)'s option (a) — D-138's warning wording is correct as written, because ascending is the reading it presumed — and with it [D-230](#d-230-l-inst-plv-class-tiebreaks-equal-precedence-warning-is-not-built-its-class-order-is)'s equal-precedence warning, which D-230 says must be built together with D-220's first clause. `domain::overlay_rules::check_replacement` holds D-138's literal condition and is now correct rather than correct-under-one-reading.
- **Propagated**: S9 §3 step 2 (`inst-plv-adjustment` — the direction stated where `fixed` is defined) and step 3a (`inst-plv-class-tiebreak` — the total order now carries *ascending*, and the note that D-220/D-230 are unblocked); §F.1's row, struck with Tariffs' adoption still owed. **§5's warning list is owed with D-220's build**, not here: its wording is what D-249 makes correct, and the two belong in one change.

#### D-250 [M] The gated-market gauge refreshes on the gear's established periodic-sweep tick — **DECIDED and BUILT 2026-08-07**

- **Where**: `infra/metrics.rs` (D-246's observable gauge), `config::JobsConfig`; D-246's *"the refresher's cadence is a value the §14 NFR set should ratify beside the gear's other bounds"*.
- **Problem**: D-246 built the instrument and the read (`price_repo::gated_markets`) and left the task that calls one into the other unbuilt, because its cadence is a ratified value rather than an implementation constant.
- **Decision (owner, 2026-08-07): 60 seconds**, a new `jobs.gated_markets_tick_secs` defaulting to `60`.
- **Chosen from the gear's own convention rather than as a fresh number.** Every `_secs` knob in `JobsConfig` is derived from a stated bound: `readmodel_warm_tick_secs: 5` and `readmodel_degraded_after_secs: 5` from §1.2's publish→read-model SLO, `catalog_version_overdue_secs: 300` from D-47's max batching delay. The closest sibling is `window_activation_tick_secs: 60`, whose reasoning transfers directly — *"a sweep an order of magnitude inside that floor cannot be what makes a changeover late, while a 5s sweep would spend twelve passes a minute reading an index for rows that are almost never there."*
- **The argument is stronger here than for windows.** A window's changeover is bounded by D-47's five-minute authoring floor, so 60s is one order inside it. This gauge's subject — a market gated on tax-category readiness — moves when an operator declares a category or the future Tax Engine answers, which the PRD's risk table sizes in **months**. 60s is therefore conservative by many orders, and the cost it avoids is larger: the refresher's read is a catalog-wide scan, not an index probe.
- **Not shorter, and stated so the next reader does not tighten it**: the value feeds §7's alarm on a condition an operator resolves in days. A tick below a minute buys no operator anything and puts a scan on the box every few seconds.
- **Built**: `jobs.gated_markets_tick_secs` (default 60, zero refused like its siblings), `infra::jobs::gated_markets::GatedMarketsJob`, and a **third ticker** in `module.rs` that runs it under its own coordination lease. With this, **D-246 is closed end to end** — it had built the instrument and the read and left nothing calling one into the other.
- **The refresher publishes nothing when it cannot read, rather than publishing `0`.** That is the same `[C]` D-246 removed, arriving from the other side: a zero on a failed read clears §7's alarm over markets that are still gated, while a stale gauge is a flat series an operator can see. Asserted by call **count** as well as value, because those two states are indistinguishable from the value alone.
- **The lease is per gear and per pass**, on `WARM_LEASE_KEY`'s reason: the quantity is catalog-wide, so one replica's read is the whole answer and every other replica reading concurrently is the same scan run N times for one number.
- **Propagated**: `PRD.md` §14's ratified-targets bullet, which now carries all four background-sweep cadences with the bound each derives from — the warm re-drive's 5s, the `CatalogVersion` overdue 300s, window activation's 60s and this one — rather than only the size and availability figures. The `JobsConfig` doc comment naming its bound is written.

#### D-251 [H] What an absent cross-gear dependency means, stated once instead of a fourth time

- **Where**: [`design/11-lifecycle.md`](./design/11-lifecycle.md) §3 `inst-cl-source`; D-182 (the D-79 lane), D-162's tier-2 note, `inst-md-entitlements` (D-252); every future slice that reaches for a store another gear owns.
- **Problem**: this repository holds one gear of a set, so a slice regularly reaches for a store, lane or registry that does not exist. Three such absences landed in three consecutive slices and each got a bespoke entry — D-182 for the D-79 subscriptions lane, D-162's note for `pricing_historical_price`, and the Contracts lock registry here. A fourth was about to be written. Bespoke entries also disagree: D-182 refuses outright, while the migration plane's built behaviour returns an empty, marked result.
- **Decision (owner, 2026-08-07): one rule, and the discriminator is what the absence can be mistaken for.**
  1. **Fail closed where the absence can produce a wrong answer.** If the missing input feeds a predicate whose "no" would let a write through — an exemption, a coverage check, a lock — the rule refuses, with the code it already declares, and the refusal does **not** distinguish "never built" from "not answering". That is D-182 clause (1) verbatim, and it stands unchanged: a lane with no client is no more informative than one that timed out, and making the safe direction depend on the *reason* for silence is how a fail-safe becomes a coin flip.
  2. **An empty result is admissible only when it is marked and does not leave the gear.** Where the absence produces an emptiness that cannot be mistaken for an answer, the surface may stand — but **both** conditions bind: the result carries an explicit unresolved marker (not a bare empty set), **and** no mounted surface hands it to a consumer that would treat it as authoritative.
  3. **The design set records the absence where the instruction lives**, in the voice D-182 set — normatively, once, rather than as a comment in whichever module met it first.
- **Why the discriminator is that one.** An empty delta report reads as *"no subscriber has a problem"* when the truth is *"no subscriber could be enumerated"*, and those are opposite facts for an operator about to confirm. What makes the difference is not how the emptiness arose but whether anything downstream can tell. Clause (2)'s two conditions are exactly the two ways it can tell: a marker on the value, and no path to a consumer.
- **Applied to `inst-cl-source`, which is what forced the rule.** The instruction says a lock-registry outage fails the mutation closed. The migration plane's **scheduling** surface stands: the Contracts registry is absent, every subscription reads locked and is excluded, `MigrationSchedule::subjects_unresolved` marks the result, and — decisively — `POST /migrations/{id}/start`, the only surface that hands an exclusion set to Subscriptions, is **not mounted**. Clause (2) is satisfied in both halves. The day the registry exists, the letter of `inst-cl-source` governs again and nothing about the surface changes.
- **What this does not license.** A mounted route that returns an empty set without a marker; a marker nobody reads; or an emptiness standing in for a predicate that guards a write. Each is clause (1)'s case wearing clause (2)'s clothes.
- **Propagated**: (2026-08-08) S11 §3 `inst-cl-source` (clause (1) is the letter of the instruction and stands unchanged; clause (2)'s two conditions named, both met — `subjects_unresolved` marks the result and `/start` is unmounted); S11 §5's `start` row (why it is built and deliberately not mounted, and that the unmounted-ness is what satisfies clause (2) for `inst-cl-source`); D-182's entry (the back-reference — it is now clause (1)'s worked example rather than a bespoke rule).

#### D-252 [M] The entitlement grant store does not exist, and the overflow judgement cannot fire without it — **PREMISE REFUTED 2026-08-08 at the merge: the store landed the same day, one strand over**

- **Where**: [`design/11-lifecycle.md`](./design/11-lifecycle.md) §3 `inst-md-entitlements`; `pricing/src/infra/migration.rs`; `m20260802_000001`'s doc comment.
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-07 by the Slice 11-migration strand, building the delta analyzer): `inst-md-entitlements` has the migration delta report flag a target whose entitlement grants do not cover what the source granted. The judgement is built and tested. **There is no grant store in this gear at all** — no table, no column, no domain type — so `target_shape` reports an empty grant set and the class has no input. It currently reads as an implemented safety check.
- **The premise is false, and it was false the moment this branch merged (measured 2026-08-08).** This entry and Slice 6 were written by **concurrent strands** against a common ancestor, and Slice 6 landed the store the same day: `m20260802_000053` adds **`pricing_plan.entitlement_grants`**, the domain type is `EntitlementGrants` (`plan_tier_ref`, a plan-level `GrantSet` of `feature_flags: BTreeMap<String, bool>` + `quotas: BTreeMap<String, i64>`, and a `per_phase` map), and `plan_repo::load_current` populates it through `to_entitlement_grants`. All three counts of *"no table, no column, no domain type"* are wrong — and the sharpest form of it is that **`target_shape` already holds the grants**: it calls `plan_repo::load_current` on its first line, receives a `PlanRevision` carrying the set, and then writes `grants: Vec::new()` under a comment stating the column does not exist. Neither branch was wrong on its own; this is visible only in the merge, which is why merges are gated here and not only branches.
- **What that changes, stated rather than left to inference.** This is **not** a [D-251](#d-251-h-what-an-absent-cross-gear-dependency-means-stated-once-instead-of-a-fourth-time) case at all — nothing cross-gear is absent, the store is *this gear's own*, and clause (2) was applied to a dependency that had already landed. What remains is ordinary owed wiring **plus one genuine question the wiring cannot answer by itself**, which is why it is not a one-line fix and is recorded rather than quietly built: the analyzer's vocabulary is `Vec<(String, i64)>` and `TargetShape::grant_of` reads *absent as zero*, so **the `quotas` half maps across exactly and the `feature_flags` half has no representation** — a flag the target drops is an entitlement loss that neither `false` nor absence can express as a total — and **`per_phase` has no operand on the subject side**, `SubscriptionFacts` carrying no phase for the analyzer to select a set by. Deciding what a lost flag and a phase-scoped set mean to the overflow class is a design act, not a repository change.
- **The subject side stays absent and that part of the record stands.** `SubscriptionFacts::grants` — *"the totals it resolves on the source"* — comes from Subscriptions, which has no crate here and enumerates nothing. So the class still cannot fire end to end after the wiring; what changes is **why**, and the surviving reason is `inst-cl-source`'s unresolved subject set (D-251 clause (2), where it genuinely applies), not an absent grant store.
- **Decision (owner, 2026-08-07): record it as an owed *dependency*, under [D-251](#d-251-h-what-an-absent-cross-gear-dependency-means-stated-once-instead-of-a-fourth-time) clause (3).** The same shape as `pricing_historical_price` one step over and the D-79 lane one slice back. No code is written against an absent store, and the judgement is left built rather than deleted: it is correct, it costs nothing, and deleting it would put the same reasoning back in a later slice's way.
- **Which D-251 clause it falls under, stated rather than left to inference: (2), narrowly** — **withdrawn 2026-08-08 by the refutation above; D-251 does not reach this entry, and the paragraph is kept only so the reasoning that was applied stays legible.** An empty grant set makes the overflow class silent, and silence here cannot be mistaken for a passing check *because the delta report carries the same unresolved marker the subject set does* and the executing route is unmounted. Were the report ever handed to a consumer as authoritative, this becomes clause (1) and the analyzer must refuse instead.
- **One false reference corrected in the same change**: `m20260802_000001`'s doc named `(plan_id, revision)` as "the name the grant table is keyed on", present tense, for a table that does not exist. Softened to the conditional — a Foundation migration asserting a table into being is how a reader concludes the store is there.
- **Propagated**: (2026-08-08) S11 §3 `inst-md-entitlements` — carrying the **corrected** account, not the refuted one: the store exists as `pricing_plan.entitlement_grants`, `target_shape` discards a set it already loads, the `feature_flags`/`per_phase` question is what makes the fix more than plumbing, and the class stays unable to fire on the **subject** side alone.
- **Owed, and it is this gear's own work rather than a dependency**: (1) `target_shape` maps the `quotas` half of the loaded `EntitlementGrants` into `TargetShape::grants` instead of `Vec::new()`, with the case living where the repository runs rather than in a domain test that hand-builds a world; (2) a decision on the `feature_flags` half and the `per_phase` axis, which the analyzer's `(String, i64)` vocabulary cannot express as written; (3) `infra/migration.rs`'s module doc and `target_shape`'s own comment both assert the absent store and must stop. **All three closed 2026-08-08**: (2) is [D-253](#d-253-m-the-entitlement-overflow-class-needed-a-vocabulary-and-two-halves-of-it-were-not-plumbing), (1) is `grant_totals` called from `target_shape` with the store case in `sqlite_plan_phase`, and (3) is done in the same commit. What remains is the **subject** side, which is `inst-cl-source`'s absent lane and never was this entry's.


#### D-253 [M] The entitlement overflow class needed a vocabulary, and two halves of it were not plumbing

- **Where**: `pricing/src/domain/migration_delta.rs` (`grant_totals`, `TargetShape::grants`); `pricing/src/infra/migration.rs` (`target_shape`); [`design/11-lifecycle.md`](./design/11-lifecycle.md) §3 `inst-md-entitlements`; [D-252](#d-252-m-the-entitlement-grant-store-does-not-exist-and-the-overflow-judgement-cannot-fire-without-it), D-41, D-19.
- **Problem** (2026-08-08, closing D-252's owed item (1)): `inst-md-entitlements` compares the target's grant totals against the source's and flags a shortfall. The analyzer's vocabulary is `Vec<(String, i64)>` and `TargetShape::grant_of` reads **absent as zero**. The stored set is `EntitlementGrants` — a `plan_tier_ref`, a plan-level `GrantSet` of `feature_flags: BTreeMap<String, bool>` **and** `quotas: BTreeMap<String, i64>`, and a `per_phase` map. The `quotas` half maps across exactly. The other two do not, and neither has an answer the mapping can derive from the types.
- **Two questions, and both are decisions rather than plumbing.** **(a) Flags.** A capability the target drops is an entitlement loss, but `false` and *absent* are not totals, and — decisively — **nothing makes the two key spaces disjoint**: `GrantSet` permits a quota and a flag of the same name, so a flat merge silently makes them one entry whose value is whichever was written second. **(b) Phases.** A phased plan grants a different set per phase and the analyzer has **no phase to select by**: `SubscriptionFacts` carries none, because the subject side comes from Subscriptions and there is no lane.
- **Decision (2026-08-08). (a) Namespaced keys, flags as `1`/`0`.** Every key carries a prefix — `quota:` or `flag:` — which removes the collision by construction and reads to an operator as well as the bare name did (`flag:beta-access`). A granted flag is `1`, a withheld or absent one `0`, so `grant_of`'s "absent is zero" and the rule's `target < source` together say the true thing about flags with no second comparison and no new type: losing a capability is `0 < 1`, gaining one is not a delta. **(b) The minimum across the plan's phases.** A migrated subscriber occupies **every** phase of the target in turn, so a phase granting less than the source did is a loss they will actually meet — whether it is the trial they enter first or the terminal phase they end in. The plan-level set alone hides exactly that; the maximum hides it louder. An **unphased** plan has no phase map by construction (D-19, `phase_map` returns empty below two phases) and is measured at its plan-level set, rather than at an empty minimum that would read as "grants nothing".
- **Why the minimum is the fail-closed direction, stated because it is the arguable half.** The failure that matters is **under**-reporting: silently migrating a subscriber into a phase with less quota than they were using is the money-visible outcome `inst-md-entitlements` exists to prevent. Over-reporting costs the operator a delta they can resolve by scoping the subscription out. A rule whose error direction is "the operator sees one more blocking delta than strictly necessary" is the one to pick where the alternative is "the operator sees none and the subscriber overflows".
- **One consequence measured rather than predicted**: an authored per-phase set **replaces** the plan-level one rather than merging with it (`inst-gs-perphase`), so a phase that authors quotas and no flags grants **no** flags — the store case for this rule was first written expecting the plan-level flag to survive into the trial and the case corrected it.
- **Owed to Subscriptions, and it is a contract rather than a courtesy**: `SubscriptionFacts::grants` is the *source* side of the same comparison and must use this vocabulary — the same prefixes and the same `1`/`0` for flags — or the two sides will compare keys that never match and the class will read as "no overflow" for every subscriber. Defined now, before a counterparty binds to anything, and added to the standing list of owed cross-gear adoptions. **The class still cannot fire end to end**: the subject side has no producer, which is `inst-cl-source`'s absent-lane problem and not this entry's.
- **Flagged for veto.** The clauses are engineering readings of an instruction that does not settle either question, but (b) changes what an operator is **shown before confirming a migration**, and the choice between "the stingiest phase" and "the phase they land in" is a product call the day a subject side exists to make it answerable. Recorded as decided-and-built so nothing waits on it, and marked so the owner can reverse it cheaply — reversing (b) is one function, and reversing (a) is a prefix.
- **One thing no test guards, found by a probe that reddened nothing**: `target_shape`'s call to `grant_totals` has **no case behind it**. Reverting it to `Vec::new()` leaves the store case green — that case composes the two repository reads with the mapping, which is the part that could silently be wrong, and does not go through `target_shape` itself. The only surface that would expose a `TargetShape` is a delta report, and that needs a subject set this gear cannot enumerate — the same absence that keeps the whole class silent. What actually catches the reversion is **clippy under `-D warnings`** (the unused import and the unused binding both become errors, measured), which is a real gate rather than a hope, but it is a lint and not an assertion. Recorded rather than closed by inventing a surface to observe it through.
- **Propagated**: (2026-08-08) S11 §3 `inst-md-entitlements` (the vocabulary, both clauses and the owed Subscriptions half).

#### D-254 [H] Slice 10's six columns never reached the materiality delta domain, so a reserved-rate rise auto-published

- **Where**: `pricing/src/domain/materiality/delta.rs` (`quantity_change`); `pricing/src/domain/rules/supersession.rs` (`mismatched_unit_fields`); [`design/10-advanced-primitives.md`](./design/10-advanced-primitives.md) §3 `inst-rv-attrs`/`inst-rv-level`/`inst-ft-typed`, §10; D-115, D-139, D-53.
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-08 by a **review** over a green tree, hours after the Slice 10 merge was judged and accepted): none of the slice's six new columns — `reserved_rate_minor`, `reservation_flavor`, `min_qty_purchase`, `min_qty_usage`, `min_qty_usage_fallback`, `discount_ref` — reached `domain/materiality`. Measured: the string `reserved_rate_minor` and its five siblings appear **nowhere** in that directory. `delta.rs`'s own module doc had already stated the obligation in the words this entry quotes back at it — *"the slice that adds each column adds its arm below"*.
- **What that cost, traced through the code rather than asserted**: `row_delta` consults `contract_change`, then `quantity_change`, then dispatches on `model_kind` to `amount_delta` / `band_delta` / `package_delta`. `quantity_change` knew only `manual_quantity` and `includedAllowance.quantity`; the dispatch arms compare `amount_minor` and the band vector. So a successor that moves **only** `reserved_rate_minor` passes both gates unchanged, reaches `amount_delta`, and yields `Amount { from: X, to: X }` — a **zero move**, which is immaterial, which publishes on **one principal**. `reserved_rate_minor` is money and D-139 denominates it *per covered granule*, so a fourfold rise in the reserved rate reached consumers with no second approver on a row whose on-demand price never moved.
- **Why no gate saw it.** The absence of an arm is invisible to every local gate: fast was 2242/0, Postgres 267/0, clippy 0, dylint 0, spec-check 233/1, and the merge itself was gated. There was no case asserting these fields are material, and a missing rule fails no test that was never written. This is the second time on this program that a read-only review over a **green** tree has produced a finding the gates structurally cannot.
- **Decision (2026-08-08): the four charge-affecting fields fail closed; the two that are not this domain's are named rather than silently omitted.**
  1. **`reserved_rate_minor` → `NotComputable`**, not an `Amount` move. It is money, but the effective delta needs the covered-granule count, which is Rating's runtime fact — so D-115 clause (2)'s G1 fail-safe applies and the operator gets a second signature instead of a number.
  2. **`reservation_flavor`, `min_qty_usage`, `min_qty_usage_fallback` → `NotComputable`.** These are exactly the three the same series filed **inside** `evaluation-policy-generation`'s roster on the ground that they decide what quantity is billable. A field the roster calls quantity-determining and this domain called immaterial is the same contradiction `includedAllowance.quantity` was added here to close, and the two classifications now agree.
  3. **`min_qty_purchase` and `discount_ref` are deliberately not arms.** The first is an order-time permission enforced by Subscriptions — it decides who may buy, not what a subscriber pays. The second is another gear's instrument that this catalog *"does not author, evaluate, or stack"* (`inst-dr-boundary`). Both are recorded here so their absence reads as a decision rather than as the same oversight repeated.
- **Second defect, same shape, same origin**: `mismatched_unit_fields` had no arm for `reservation_flavor`, so a mid-window supersession flipping `capacity → consumption` inherited a **continued** tier counter accumulated under the other reading — under `capacity` the charge never touches `Q` (`inst-rv-level`), under `consumption` the matched quantity is excluded and the remainder's `Q` restarts at zero (`inst-rv-tier-q`). Added as **this guard's own** field rather than to the shared seven of `unit_determining_mismatch`, and the split is the decision: that list is also `inst-ph-override-units`', and widening it would rebind a phase-scoped override on an argument the design set has not made.
- **What this says about acceptance.** The Slice 10 hand-back was judged by measurement, its central refutation was verified, its one self-declared guard weakening was proved minimal, and the merge was gated — and this still got through, because every one of those checks asks *"is what was built correct?"* and none asks *"is anything that should have been built missing?"* The review lens is the one that asks the second question, and it is owed on every merge rather than on the ones that feel risky.
- **Propagated**: (2026-08-08) S10 §10 (the materiality line, which named reserved-rate changes as price mutations and had no enforcement behind it).

#### D-255 [L] Two smaller review findings on the same wave: a discard with no compare-and-swap, and a doc that claimed a check nobody wrote

- **Where**: `pricing/src/infra/storage/repo/overlay_repo.rs` (`abandon_draft`); `pricing/src/domain/price_row.rs` (`discount_ref`); D-231, D-254, `inst-dr-referential`.
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-08 by the same review that produced D-254, and both are `[L]` because neither is reachable from a mounted route today):
  1. **`OverlayRepo::abandon_draft` took no `expected` row version**, unlike `PlanRepo::abandon_draft`, so a caller holding a tag from before another author's edit could discard that edit. A discard is **not remediable** — since D-231 the revision becomes a terminal tombstone and its content is gone — which is why the plane one table over has always guarded it.
  2. **`PriceRow::discount_ref`'s doc said "this gear validates that it resolves"**, which is the opposite of what S10 §5 and `m20260802_000056` both record: there is no Promotions or Tariffs registry client anywhere in this gear, `DISCOUNT_REF_UNRESOLVED` is declared and unraisable, and an unresolvable ref publishes and freezes into the snapshot unchecked. A field's own doc is the account a caller is most likely to trust, so it was the worst of the three places to be wrong.
- **Decision (2026-08-08): both corrected.** `abandon_draft` gains `expected` and the compare-and-swap **is** the guard, on `replace_lines`' shape one method over — and the bump happens **before** the line drop, so no write is issued on the way to saying no (`publish_revision_on`'s ordering). The doc now says the field is persisted and not validated, and names why.
- **One shape named rather than guarded against**: abandoning revision 0 of a **never-published** overlay **spends the identity**. `open_revision` mints a successor from the *published* revision, so an overlay whose only revision was discarded has none to open from. That is the correct outcome for an overlay no consumer ever saw — nothing pinned it, nothing resolved it, and a fresh `create` costs one id — and a resurrection path would be surface the design set has not asked for. Recorded because "discard" reads to a caller as "undo", and this is not that.

#### D-256 [M] The composite meter lands as one group, because a revision-scoped table cannot land as half of one

- **Where**: `m20260802_000046_create_pricing_composite_meter`; `pricing/src/domain/plan_shape.rs` (`CompositeMeter`); `pricing/src/domain/plan_rules/composite.rs`; `pricing/src/infra/storage/repo/plan_shape_repo.rs`; `pricing/src/domain/approval/content_pin.rs`; [`design/10-advanced-primitives.md`](./design/10-advanced-primitives.md) §3 `inst-cm-constituents`/`inst-cm-formula`/`inst-cm-frozen`/`inst-cm-output`, §6; A4, D-32, D-106.
- **Problem**: `pricing_composite_meter` was one of two tables §6 names that did not exist. A first attempt built the table and its six census entries and was **reverted rather than committed**, because `sqlite_plan_repo::the_revision_scoped_tables_are_a_closed_set_and_each_one_is_copied_and_dropped` reddened with the obligation spelled out — *"`open_revision` must copy its rows … `abandon_draft` must delete them BEFORE it flips … Do NOT simply add it here: this assertion is the notice, not the obligation."* That is D-106's copy-on-new-revision discipline enforced structurally, and it is why this is one group and not three.
- **Decision (2026-08-08): built whole.** The table (both engines, `pricing_plan_phase`'s append-only shape), the entity, `CompositeMeter` on `PlanShape`, a public `replace_composites`/`list_composites` pair, the four `plan_shape_repo` statements, the copier and dropper wired into `open_revision` and `abandon_draft` **in the required order**, the two shape cases extended, and only then the roster line.
- **Two rules built and one deliberately absent.** `CompositeArity` (`COMPOSITE_TOO_FEW_CONSTITUENTS`) and `CompositeSelfReference` (`COMPOSITE_SELF_REFERENCE`) are **`PlanShape` rules and not row-local ones**, and that is the design call: §9 asks for **transitive** self-reference, which is a cycle across two definitions that no single row can see. `COMPOSITE_CONSTITUENT_UNPUBLISHED` is declared in §5 and **raised nowhere** — `metering_unit`/`MeteringUnit` appear nowhere in `src/`, so a rule over an empty lookup either refuses every composite ever authored or passes every one, and a rule that always passes is indistinguishable from one that holds. Named exactly as `plan_rules` names `SKU_NOT_PUBLISHED`. `inst-cm-output-unit` is not this gear's act at all (D-32).
- **Arity and self-reference are publish rules, not column constraints**, inheriting `m20260802_000045`'s portability argument: `SQLite` has no incremental table-`CHECK`, a Postgres-only `ALTER` splits the two `EXPECTED_CHECKS` censuses, and `json_array_length` is an extension function whose absence would degrade a constraint silently on one engine. §6 already said so for self-reference; arity joins it.
- **`inst-cm-frozen` costs a content-pin bump, v10 → v11.** The definition freezes into the snapshot, so it is authored draft content a `PATCH` moves and it enters the preimage. The hole it closes is the formula's: a reviewer who approved `vCPU + RAM` weighted 1:1 and a commit that publishes 1:4, with every digest equal. The formula is framed as its **canonical JSON text** — this crate never reads inside it (A4), and a structural walk would be a second spelling of a value the store holds as one string.
- **One census is a subset check and was silently escaping**: `EXPECTED_TABLES` in `sqlite_migrations.rs` is *iterated*, not compared, so a new table passes it and skips the down/up round-trip coverage at its two other call sites. Adding the table there is real coverage rather than bookkeeping; the other five censuses are exact and reddened on their own.
- **Propagated**: (2026-08-08) S10 §3 (`inst-cm-constituents`/`inst-cm-formula` — the built halves and the blocked one), §6 (the publish-rule note).

#### D-257 [H] The composite rules were registered, tested and green over a field nothing ever populated

- **Where**: `pricing/src/infra/publish.rs` (`assemble_from`); `pricing/src/domain/plan_shape.rs` (`PlanShape::composites`); D-256, D-254.
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-08 by a **review** over the green tree D-256 had just produced, one commit later): `assemble_from` never set `PlanShape::composites`, and no site in `src/` ever did. `CompositeArity` and `CompositeSelfReference` were registered in the Slice-2 pipeline, unit-tested and passing — over an **empty vec**, on every publish. A one-constituent composite, or a `vm → pod → vm` cycle, published unrejected; and the v11 content pin framed a count of zero, so a formula edit moved no digest. **Both of D-256's headline claims were false in the deployed path**: the two rules did not guard, and the pin did not close the "approve 1:1, publish 1:4 with an equal digest" hole its own doc says it closes.
- **This is D-254's defect class, a second time and inside the fix for it.** D-254 was *"the slice that adds each column adds its arm below"* — a rule with no arm. This is a rule with an arm and no operand. Both pass every gate, because a rule that always passes is indistinguishable from a rule that holds, and neither absence fails a test that was never written. The unit cases were the trap: they hand-built a `PlanShape`, set `composites` themselves, and proved the arithmetic while saying nothing about whether anything fills the field — which is the same "a domain test that hand-builds a world says nothing about what the store assembles" this program has now recorded three times.
- **Decision (2026-08-08): `assemble_from` loads the composite set, and the case that proves it runs through `precheck`** — the same assembly a commit performs — rather than over a shape a test constructed. Probed: reverting the one line reddens it.
- **Four smaller findings from the same review, all corrected**: `CompositeMeter` was inserted between `PlanPhase`'s doc comment and `PlanPhase`, so it stole the doc and left `PlanPhase` undocumented; `two_composites` did the same to `published_plan_with_shape` in the test file; the two minted codes were absent from `plan_rules_tests::DECLARED`, so nothing checked their spelling against the design set byte for byte; and a spec-check comment still read "21 + 36" above an assertion lowered to 54.
- **What this says about the method, stated because it is the third time**: the gate cycle and the probes both ask *"is what was built correct?"*. Review is the only lens that asks *"is anything that should have been built missing?"*, and it has now caught three defects of exactly that shape in one day — D-254's missing arms, D-255's missing compare-and-swap, and this. It is owed on **every** merge, including the ones that close a review finding.

#### D-258 [H] The same unassembled-field defect, twice more in the same function — and one of them was a live wrong answer

- **Where**: `pricing/src/infra/publish.rs` (`assemble_from`); D-257, D-254; `inst-pc-*`, `inst-gs-perphase`.
- **Problem** (found 2026-08-08 by running D-257's own lens one field further, in the review of D-257 itself): `assemble_from` also never copied **`change_contract`** or **`entitlement_grants`** off the draft. Both are PATCH-authorable, both are framed by the content pin, both are rendered in the reviewer's document, and neither reached the shape the rules judge.
  - **`change_contract` was a live wrong answer, not only a silent gap.** `ChangeGraphAuthorable` judged `PlanChangeContract::default()`, so the change-target and comparability-rank refusals could never fire — and `COMPARABILITY_RANK_REVOKED` fired **spuriously**, because inbound edges are read off the stored column independently of the shape. Any plan another published plan pointed at was refused publication **even when it carried a rank**. That is an operator seeing a refusal for a rule they satisfied.
  - **`entitlement_grants`** left `GrantSetPhasesKnown` returning early on every publish and the pin framing the default, so the *"approved a trial capped at 20, published 20 000, with an equal digest"* hole `content_pin`'s own doc claims to close was open.
- **Decision (2026-08-08): both copied off the draft**, beside D-257's composite load, in the same three-line region that has now been wrong three times.
- **Three defects, one shape, and the shape is worth naming**: `assemble_from` is where a `PlanShape` is built from storage, and **every field added to `PlanShape` owes a line there**. Nothing enforces it — the struct is built with `PlanShape::new` and then populated field by field, so a missing line is not a compile error and no test that hand-builds a shape can see it. D-254 was a rule with no arm; D-257 and this are rules with arms and no operands. All three passed every gate.
- **Owed, and stated rather than quietly skipped — paid 2026-08-08**: the correction landed **gated but under-tested**, with the full tier green (2251/0) and no case asserting either field reaches the shape, which is the same weakness that let all three land. Both `precheck`-level cases now exist beside D-257's, in `pricing/tests/sqlite_publish_commit.rs`: a per-phase grant set keyed to a phase the schedule does not have is refused, and a ranked plan another published plan points at **publishes**. Probed one at a time by dropping each field's assignment from the assembly — each drop reddens its own case and only its own.
  - **The grant-set case needed a two-phase plan, and that is a property of the rule rather than of the fixture.** `GrantSetPhasesKnown` answers a **non-phased** plan from its first arm and returns, so on D-19's single implicit terminal phase the unknown-key walk is unreachable and the same code would have been raised by the wrong arm. The seed therefore gains a trial phase and a second recurring row to cover it, and the clean pre-check ahead of the patch is what proves the phase set reached the shape too.
  - **The second case asserts a publish, not a refusal**, because the defect made a *passing* subject fail: D-54's reverse guard reads the rank off the shape and the inbound edges off the store, so a defaulted shape refused every plan anyone pointed at. Its probe doubles as the proof that the case is not vacuous — the refusal it produces names the referring plan, so the inbound edges are a real operand and not an empty set passing for one.
- **A structural remedy is owed and is the real fix**: a destructuring assembly (`let PlanRevision { .. } = draft;` with every field named, as `content_pin::put_plan_shape` already does) would make a forgotten field a **compile error** instead of a silent default. That is what has actually been protecting the pin all along, and it is why the pin's census caught nothing here — it destructures `PlanShape`, not the draft the shape is assembled from.

#### D-259 [M] The structural remedy for the assembly defect, and the tail of the review that found it three times

- **Where**: `pricing/src/infra/publish.rs` (`assemble_from`); `pricing/src/api/rest/approvals.rs` (`PinnedContentView`); `pricing/src/domain/approval/content_pin_tests.rs` (the mutator census); D-256, D-257, D-258.
- **Decision (2026-08-08): `assemble_from` destructures the draft.** Every field of `PlanRevision` is named — the five that are deliberately not carried (`plan_id` and `revision`, the constructor's own arguments; `lifecycle_state`, `created_by`, `created_at_utc`, `row_version`, the row's provenance rather than its authored content) are ignored **by name**. A field added to `PlanRevision` and forgotten here is now `error[E0027]: pattern does not mention field`, which is the compile error that would have caught all three of D-257 and D-258's defects the day each column landed. Probed: adding a field to `PlanRevision` fails the build.
- **Why this is the real fix and the three arms were not**: `content_pin::put_plan_shape` has always destructured, which is why the pin's own coverage never drifted — and it is exactly why the pin's census caught none of these, because it destructures the **shape**, not the draft the shape is assembled from. The invariant was being enforced one layer away from where it was being broken.
- **Three tail findings from the same review, all closed**: composites were hashed by the pin and **dropped from the reviewer's document**, so a signature covered a weighting the reviewer was never shown — `PinnedContentView` now carries a `CompositeMeterView`, on this module's stated invariant that it shows *"exactly the fields the pin hashes and no others"*; the pin's mutator census had **no composite entry at all**, so D-256's framing was asserted by nothing and could have been deleted with the suite green — four mutators added, one per framed member, and the base fixture now carries a composite so the indexed ones have a member to move (the frozen digest re-pinned accordingly); and `plan_rules_tests::DECLARED`'s header anchored every spelling to `02-plan-definition.md` §5 while two of its codes live in `10-advanced-primitives.md` §5.
- **One thing this wave nearly did to the debt register, caught by the checker**: D-258's prose named a Slice 6 error code in backticks, and a bare code token in register prose **pays a P3 `code-unreferenced` debt the raising rule never earned** — the check counts any mention anywhere in the corpus. The entry now names the refusal in words instead, so the code stays in the debt it belongs in. The pin was not moved to accommodate it.
- **The neighbourhood pins moved a third time in one day** and are re-pinned again, unchanged in mechanism: the same two requirements crossing the 0.600 selection floor on prose that touched neither them nor their accounts.

#### D-260 [M] Slice 12 starts at the bulk-operation record, and its state machine is in the store

- **Where**: `m20260802_000047_create_pricing_bulk_operation`; `pricing/src/infra/storage/entity/bulk_operation.rs`; `pricing/tests/sqlite_bulk_operation_store.rs`; [`design/12-operator-efficiency.md`](./design/12-operator-efficiency.md) §4, §6, `inst-bs-*`; D-37, D-137, O4.
- **Problem**: Slice 12 was entirely unbuilt and both of its flows — bulk import and mass repricing — are one record moving through §4's six states, so nothing else in the slice could be written first. **Measured before starting, because the obvious count is wrong here**: `inst-cl-*` names two unrelated families, and all 19 of its crate mentions are Slice 11's contract-lock (`inst-cl-source`/`exclude`/`reject`/`scope`); Slice 12's clone family had one. `inst-mr-*`, `inst-bs-*` and `inst-bi-*` were at **zero**, and `pricing_bulk*` appeared nowhere in the migration roster.
- **Decision (2026-08-08): the record and its state machine, as the slice's first group.** Six states, §4's five edges enforced by the table's trigger on both engines, and three `CHECK`s beside the vocabularies.
- **`awaiting_approval` is unreachable for `kind = import`, and the store says so** rather than leaving it to a caller. D-137 makes draft-plane authoring never material, so an import parked there would be waiting for a decision nothing can ever produce — the one edge of §4 whose violation strands a record **forever**, which is why it is a `CHECK` and not a convention.
- **The three terminal states are terminal.** A record that left `completed`, `completed_with_conflicts` or `validation_failed` would re-drive a commit whose rows are already applied; the journal is the idempotency spine precisely because a re-drive must be safe, and a terminal record re-entering `committing` is not a re-drive but a second run wearing the first one's id.
- **Identity and provenance are frozen; only `state`, `report` and `completed_at` move** — a run's kind, key and submitter are what its report is *about*. And `DELETE` is refused in every state: a run is a record, not a draft.
- **It is revision-independent**, so unlike `pricing_composite_meter` (D-256) it owes **no** copy-forward and no drop-on-abandon, and the closed-set guard does not reach it. That is the one way this table was cheaper than the last.
- **Ten rosters moved, and all ten were reached** (the commit that landed this said nine and miscounted its own diff): the six SQLite censuses and the four Postgres ones (CHECK, trigger-function, trigger-name, key) that D-256's first attempt missed entirely. All four are ordered alphabetically, and `bulk` sorts before `bundle` — three of the four needed re-placing after a first pass put them before `composite` instead.
- **The state machine had no `INSERT` arm for one commit** (found by review, corrected here). `BEFORE UPDATE OR DELETE` on Postgres and no `BEFORE INSERT` on `SQLite` left a run free to be **born** in any state: born `committing` skips the approval gate transitions 2 and 3 exist to impose, and born `completed` reports outcomes for rows it never committed — the very move `a_terminal_run_never_moves_again` proves is refused as an *update*. `m20260802_000015` has carried the same arm since Slice 5 and states the reason in the same words. Every case in the new suite seeded `validating`, so the whole green suite could not see it — which is the fifth time in one day that a guard's **uncovered path** passed every gate.
- **Two findings recorded and not fixed here**, both real and neither this group's to settle:
  1. **`awaiting_approval` has no rejection exit.** §4 draws none, so a rejected batch approval strands a repricing run permanently — the only edge out is `→ committing` on approval, `DELETE` is refused, and `uq_..._client_key` stops the operator resubmitting under the same key. The origin is the design set, not this table, but the store now hard-codes it. It wants a decision of D-231's kind.
  2. **The Postgres arm was executed by nothing — closed 2026-08-08.** The behavioural suite was `SQLite`-only and Postgres was asserted by name, so dropping a disjunct from the PL/pgSQL edge list kept every gate green while production refused every conflicted run. `postgres_schema_bulk_operation` now drives the machine on the shipping engine, and the probe is the finding demonstrated: removing `completed_with_conflicts` from the `committing` disjunct leaves the **`SQLite` tier green at 8/8** and reddens Postgres. **`pricing_composite_meter` closed the same day** by `postgres_schema_composite_meter`, on the same demonstration: disabling the PL/pgSQL `UPDATE`/`DELETE` arm leaves the `SQLite` tier green at 10/10 and reddens Postgres. The debt is paid for both tables; what a new table owes from now on is a **behavioural** Postgres suite and not only four roster lines.
- **Not in this group and each owed as its own**: `pricing_repricing_journal` (the `(run_id, price_id)` idempotency spine), `pricing_bulk_row_lock` (the side table the Foundation's concurrent-edit check reads — deliberately not a column on `pricing_price`), the import parser, the repricing engine, the clone path and history/export.

#### D-261 [M] Slice 12's idempotency spine, and one `CHECK` that read right and admitted a half-set

- **Where**: `m20260802_000048_create_pricing_repricing_journal`; `pricing/src/infra/storage/entity/repricing_journal.rs`; `pricing/tests/sqlite_repricing_journal_store.rs`; `pricing/tests/postgres_schema_repricing_journal.rs`; [`design/12-operator-efficiency.md`](./design/12-operator-efficiency.md) §6, `inst-mr-journal`, `inst-mp-journal`, `inst-mp-standard`, O3; D-134, D-260.
- **Problem**: a mass repricing run has to survive a crash mid-apply, and the only thing that can make a re-drive safe is a per-row record that commits **inside the same transaction** as the successor row and its outbox event. Nothing in Slice 12 that touches published prices could be written before it existed.
- **Decision (2026-08-08): the journal as the slice's second group**, keyed `(run_id, price_id)` — which is also the dedup key the run's events carry, so the row and the event about it name one identity rather than two that have to be kept in step.
- **A journal row is born `pending`, and on this table that arm is worth more than the edges are.** D-260's missing `INSERT` arm let a run be born past its own machine; the same hole here is worse in kind, because a row born `applied` is a row the re-drive **skips** — the operator sees a completed run, the price was never touched, and no state anywhere disagrees. Probed on both engines: dropping the arm reddens only the tier it was dropped from.
- **A decided row never moves again, written as one guard rather than three.** `OLD.state <> 'pending'` refuses both terminal states *and* freezes the outcome columns, so an `applied` row cannot later be made to name a different successor than the one that committed. Deliberately not spelled as a disjunct list of the two sanctioned edges: the vocabulary `CHECK` already limits `state` to three values, so an edge list out of `pending` could not fail — and a rule that cannot fail is indistinguishable from a rule that holds.
- **The `applied` agreement is stated per column, and the pair-wise spelling it replaced was the session's recurring defect in a new dress.** `(state = 'applied') = (applied_price_id IS NOT NULL AND applied_at IS NOT NULL)` reads correctly and admits a **pending** row carrying a successor id with a null instant — the re-drive would then apply that row a second time. Found by re-reading the constraint before gating rather than by a test, because no test asked for the half-set shape; the case that distinguishes the two spellings was added with the fix and reddens on both engines when the weaker form is restored.
- **`not-attempted` is not a fourth state.** `inst-bs-abort` reports an aborted run's uncommitted rows with that word; it is how the **report** renders a row this table holds as `pending`. The consequence is deliberate: an aborted run leaves `pending` rows behind forever, so nothing here requires a terminal run's journal to be free of them, and §6's "a run is complete when no `pending` rows remain" is a statement about the runner's work rather than about the operation's state.
- **Two things this group declined to build, each for the same reason.** No `plan_id`, though D-134 commits per plan: §6 specifies four columns beside the key, a plan is reachable from `price_id`, and denormalizing it would be this group inventing a column for a group not yet written. No secondary index: every read is run-scoped and the key already leads with `run_id`, while the one genuinely cross-run question — *is this price inside an in-flight run* — is `pricing_bulk_row_lock`'s, which is much of why §6 makes the lock its own table.
- **Nine roster sites, not ten**, and the missing one is the point: the six `SQLite` censuses and the four Postgres ones minus the index roster, because this table has no secondary index. The four Postgres roster lines were reached first this time rather than after a correction.
- **The `CHECK` on the state vocabulary is not observable at `INSERT`**, and the suites say so rather than asserting it there. The born-pending arm is strictly narrower and answers first, so a case expecting the constraint at insert would be reading the trigger's refusal instead — the same shadowing `postgres_schema_composite_meter` recorded for its parent key, met here a second time and asserted through the update path where the constraint is reachable.
- **`EXPECTED_TABLES` was probed rather than trusted**: with the new entry removed, the whole `SQLite` census suite stays green at 10/10. The subset-check claim holds, and the entry buys the down/up round-trip coverage rather than any detection.
- **Six corrections from the review of the commit that landed it** (2026-08-08, the same lens D-260's INSERT arm came out of, and again a commit later):
  1. **`price_id` and `applied_price_id` were unkeyed.** Both sibling tables carrying a `price_id` — `pricing_price_tier_band` and `pricing_price_window` — key theirs, and the omission was not a decision: a journal row naming a price that does not exist is `pending` forever, the re-drive can never apply it, the run's completion predicate is never satisfied, and the only signal is the stalled-run alarm, which cannot be told apart from a dead runner. The module doc's own **"what this table deliberately does not carry"** section listed three omissions and was silent on this one, which is how a gap passes for a choice.
  2. **The entity claimed a `kind` the schema did not hold.** "a `pricing_bulk_operation` of `kind = repricing`" was prose over a foreign key that admits `import` just as happily. Enforced rather than deleted, because §6 and `inst-bi-commit` put an import's per-row outcomes in the operation's own report and nothing drives an import through this table: a row here under an import is a record no code will ever complete. **A claim in a doc comment that the schema does not hold is the same defect class as a rule with no operand** — it reads as a guarantee and is not one.
  3. **Both parent-reading arms now defer to their foreign key when the run is absent.** A `BEFORE` trigger answers ahead of the key on either engine, so the new `kind` arm would have fired for a row naming *no* run — reporting a fault the caller does not have, and leaving the key unobservable and therefore unassertable. This is `pricing_composite_meter`'s recorded shadowing met a third time, and the first time it has been **designed out** rather than documented: one `EXISTS` conjunct per arm.
  4. **The `SQLite` suite asserted the bare word `CHECK`** where the house standard, stated in `tests/common/mod.rs` and followed by `sqlite_window_guards`, is the constraint name — which `SQLite` does carry. Inside one case four constraints were indistinguishable, so a rewrite that let a *different* one answer would have stayed green. Now named on both engines.
  5. **Three properties had no behavioural case at all**, the primary key among them — the uniqueness that makes this a spine rather than a log, declared in `EXPECTED_PRIMARY_KEYS` on both engines and refused by nothing anyone had asked. Added, with the cross-state shapes (`applied` carrying a failure reason) and the mirror half-set (an instant with no successor).
  6. **A false premise in a test's own doc comment**, wrong in all three of its halves: `PRAGMA foreign_keys` is **on** by default — `sqlx` puts it in its default pragma set — `toolkit_db` never touches it (its parser handles `journal_mode` and `synchronous` only), and `sqlite_bundle_store` sets it for the **opposite** reason, to keep a `must_succeed` asserting the *absence* of a key from going vacuous. A `must_be_rejected` needs no pragma. The pragma line is gone and the premise is stated correctly, because the next reader would have acted on it.

#### D-262 [M] The bulk lock, as a side table — and the one rule this table must *not* have

- **Where**: `m20260802_000049_create_pricing_bulk_row_lock`; `pricing/src/infra/storage/entity/bulk_row_lock.rs`; `pricing/tests/sqlite_bulk_row_lock_store.rs`; `pricing/tests/postgres_schema_bulk_row_lock.rs`; [`design/12-operator-efficiency.md`](./design/12-operator-efficiency.md) §6, `inst-bk-lock`, `inst-bs-commit`, `inst-bs-approval`; D-37, Foundation `fr-concurrent-edit`; D-260, D-261.
- **Decision (2026-08-08): Slice 12's third group is the lock, keyed `(tenant_id, price_id)`** — and that key **is** the mutual exclusion, since it admits one lock per row per tenant and a second run's insert collides. The row carries the operation because naming the holder is the whole point: the interactive editor's conflict has to say *which* run holds the row.
- **A side table and not a column, because the column was never available.** The 2026-07-31 review fix said so and this build confirms why: a repricing run locks **published** rows, and `pricing_price`'s append-only trigger whitelists exactly two `UPDATE`s on one — the `lifecycle_state` flip and a monotonic `grandfather_until` tightening. A lock marker on the row is refused by that trigger, so the earlier "nullable column **or** a side table, implementation choice" offered an option that cannot be built.
- **`DELETE` is unguarded, and that is the rule rather than an omission.** Both sibling tables of this slice refuse `DELETE` outright and copying them here would be exactly wrong: release *is* a delete. Two grounds, **both from the design set**: §6 orders the operator abort as one act — `committing → completed_with_conflicts` with "the lock cleared" — so a guard admitting `DELETE` only under a `committing` run would refuse the clear inside the very transaction §6 specifies, depending on which statement ran first; and a crash between the terminal transition and the release strands the locks under a terminal run, where such a guard makes them unreleasable **permanently** — the freeze D-37 exists to prevent. The trigger is bound to `INSERT OR UPDATE` and **not** to `DELETE`, so the absence is structural rather than a fall-through nobody notices.
- **The first version of that bullet justified the rule with a release actor the design set does not have** (a "janitor" cleaning up after a crash; found by review, and the word appeared nowhere in the corpus but here). The rule was right and its stated reason was invented — and the invention did not even carry the conclusion, since a crashed runner leaves its run in `committing` and the lease takeover re-drives it there. This is the session's recurring failure in its documentary form: **a justification that names something no design clause supplies is a rule with no operand, written in prose.** The two grounds above are the ones the design set actually gives, and one of the four places carrying the claim — the Postgres suite — had already stated it correctly.
- **A probe that reddened nothing, and it was a finding.** Binding the **Postgres** trigger to `DELETE` as well left every case green — because a PL/pgSQL `BEFORE DELETE` trigger returning `NEW` returns `NULL`, which **silently cancels** the delete. No error is raised, so a case asserting only "the statement did not fail" cannot tell a release from a release that never happened. The Postgres suite did not count the rows back; it does now, and the probe reddens two cases. The production consequence was the precise failure this table's release path exists to prevent: locks never released, interactive authoring frozen behind a dead run.
- **That finding was first written down as "every case on both engines", and it was not** (corrected on review). The probe changed one statement, the Postgres trigger binding, and the `SQLite` tier was green because **nothing on it had been touched** — not because the same mechanism applies. It cannot: `SQLite` triggers have no return value and cannot cancel a statement, a `SQLite` `BEFORE DELETE` trigger carrying either `INSERT` arm's `NEW.bulk_operation_id` fails at `CREATE TRIGGER` outright, and any added trigger reddens `EXPECTED_TRIGGERS`, which asserts in both directions. **A green tier that the probe never touched is not evidence about that tier** — the same shape as a rule that is never asked, arriving in the reading of a result rather than in the code.
- **A lock may be taken only under a run that is `committing`**, and §4 is explicit in both directions — `inst-bs-commit` starts the lock on entry, `inst-bs-approval` says rows are **not** locked while a run awaits approval, so a lock taken early bounces interactive supersessions on keys the design leaves free.
- **A lock is taken or released, never edited.** `UPDATE` is refused outright: an in-place hand-off would tell the interactive editor that an operation which never took the row holds it, and leave the release path — which keys on the operation — unable to find what it owns.
- **D-261's review lessons were applied here before they could recur**: the lock keys `price_id` as well as its run (a lock on a price that does not exist is one the concurrent-edit check never reads and the release sweep never notices); both parent-reading arms **defer to their foreign key** when the run is absent, so neither reports a tenancy or state fault for a run that simply is not there; the keys are asserted one referent at a time, because `SQLite` names neither constraint nor table in a key violation and a case removing two referents at once would prove neither; and no test carries the false `PRAGMA foreign_keys` premise.
- **Eight roster sites, not ten**: this table has **no `CHECK` constraints at all**, so both `CHECK` censuses take nothing. Every rule it has is about custody rather than about columns agreeing with each other, which is what makes it different in kind from the slice's other two tables. The three trigger-body digests were read off the built schema **twice** — the two parent-reading arms gained their deferral after the first reading and both digests moved, so a hand-derived pin would have frozen the shape from before the correction.
- **One index, and it earns its place** where `pricing_repricing_journal`'s would not have: this table is keyed for the **reader** (the concurrent-edit check, arriving with a price id) and swept by the **writer** (release, arriving with an operation), so `(tenant_id, bulk_operation_id)` is a second access path rather than a duplicate of the key.
- **The absence of a `kind` restriction is load-bearing and is now pinned** (found by review). `pricing_repricing_journal` carries an arm admitting only `kind = repricing`, and the symmetry is a trap: `inst-bk-lock` is the **import's** own rule — "rows in an in-flight import are marked" — and `inst-bs-commit` puts *every* import on the edge into `committing`. Every fixture in both suites seeded a repricing run, so an arm copied here for symmetry with the sibling would have left both suites green, taken its roster line as a matter of course, and left **every bulk import unable to take its lock at commit**. One `must_succeed` case per engine now locks under an `import` run; probed with exactly that copied arm, and it reddens only that case, on both engines.
- **Three narrower findings from the same review, recorded rather than built**: the two `INSERT` arms are evaluated in a fixed order on Postgres and in an engine-**undefined** order on `SQLite`, so a lock that is both another tenant's and not `committing` always reports the tenant fault on Postgres and either on the mirror — message-only, both refuse; `INSERT OR REPLACE` on `SQLite` is a `DELETE`+`INSERT` and so slips past the never-edited rule that Postgres's `ON CONFLICT … DO UPDATE` hits, which is narrow because `SeaORM` emits the latter but is a rule the mirror does not hold; and the lock's `price_id` is not bound to its `tenant_id` — a `committing` run of one tenant may mark another tenant's price row, both arms passing and the key being tenant-blind. The third is the same gap `repricing_journal` documents deliberately, and it is recorded here because this table's tenant arm invites being read as "the lock is on this tenant's row", which it is not.

#### D-263 [H] A published revision's grant set and change contract were mutable in place, and the census that guards that class could not see it

- **Where**: `m20260802_000058_guard_pricing_plan_later_columns`; `m20260802_000052`, `m20260802_000053`, `m20260802_000001`; `pricing/tests/sqlite_plan_guards.rs`, `pricing/tests/postgres_schema_plan.rs`; Foundation §3.7; D-41, D-113, D-23, D-258, D-259.
- **Problem (found 2026-08-08 while measuring an unrelated group's premises, and measured rather than inferred)**: `m20260802_000052` added `allowed_change_targets`, `comparability_rank` and `usage_counter_on_plan_change`; `m20260802_000053` added `entitlement_grants`. Neither put its columns into either engine's frozen-column guard, so on a **published** plan revision all four were movable and the frozen-column arm said nothing.
- **The consequence is the one the pin exists to prevent.** All four are inside the approval content pin — `assemble_from` destructures the draft so they reach the shape the rules judge (D-258, D-259) and `content_pin::put_plan_shape` hashes them. The digest is computed **at publish**, and these columns were mutable **after** it, so a published revision could be made to publish a different entitlement than the one its approval signed **with the digest unchanged**. The projector's warm re-drive (§4.4) then re-materializes the moved value under a frozen `CatalogVersion`.
- **Decision (2026-08-08): the guard is restated with all four**, as `m20260802_000058`, following `m20260802_000040`'s rule that a guard restatement is its own migration and `m20260802_000051`'s rule that it is produced **neither by hand nor by a free-form script**: both blocks were read out of `m20260802_000001`'s own `UP` text and the generator asserted, before writing, exactly one frozen-column arm per engine, exactly four lines added per arm, each column appearing exactly once, and every original line surviving.
- **Why nothing noticed, and this is the part worth keeping**: **both** engines' census tests enumerate the *trigger*. `a_published_revision_freezes_every_column_the_whitelist_names` pins an 18-entry array copied from the guard, and its Postgres twin hand-maintains an 18-move list whose own doc warns that "a whitelist maintained by hand rots one forgotten `OR` at a time". **A census that enumerates the guard is structurally blind to what the guard omits** — it proves the named columns are frozen and can never report a column nobody named.
